\mathcal{A}
making
T
queries to a Haar random unitary
U
and
k\in[N]
, if
\mathcal{A}
solves
(1+\delta)-\mathrm{XHOG}
, then there exists a state
\psi
which is
O(Tk^{-1/2})
-close to
\mathcal{A}
’s output classical-quantum state such that

H(Z|UE)_{\psi}\geq\left(\delta-O(k^{3}/N)-O(nTk^{-1/2}+nTN^{-1/2})\right)n-\log_{2}n-2\log_{2}k-O(1).

(III.2)

Although this only provides asymptotic entropy, the exact bound can be obtained by tracking all the constants carefully, which results in

H(Z|UE)_{\psi}\geq\left(\delta-\frac{(k+1)k(k-1)}{N}-2\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3)\right)n-\log_{2}n-2\log_{2}k-O(1),

(III.3)

where

\varepsilon^{\prime}=T\left(\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{k}\right)^{k/2}\right)}+2/\sqrt{k/2+1}\right).

(III.4)

The parameter
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is the bound on the diamond distance between the actual algorithm
\mathcal{A}
and an algorithm
\mathcal{F}
, where
\mathcal{F}
only uses
k
samples drawn from the bitstring probability distribution induced by the circuit as input. We reserve the notation
\varepsilon
for later use as the smoothing parameter of the entropy. The second term of Eq.
III.4
in the parenthesis is due to Lemma 41, and the third term is due to Lemma 42 of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
. They propagate to Theorem 3 of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
and is paraphrased as Theorem 7.4 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
. The factor of
2
is due to setting
n
of Lemma 41 and
m
of Lemma 42 of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
to
k/2
, so a total of
m+n=k
copies of the state is needed. The cubic term in
k
of the entropy is due to Theorem 7.10 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, which we can carefully calculate.

The above analysis is not sufficient. Specifically, protocols may submit
m
circuits in a batch, which may be relevant for fast quantum devices whose classical communication latency dominates compared to the shot time, so the analysis for the query-access quantum adversary needs to be generalized. Another reason may be amortization of near-constant overhead per submission such as circuit compilation. We may also be interested in parallel execution of challenge circuits by the prover to reduce the average time per sample. Under our protocol where each round sends
m
challenges circuits at once, the single-round entropy needs to scale with
m
, which is not obvious from the analysis of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
. We therefore provide a proof of this claim.

Since the original result is based on solving the
\mathrm{XHOG}
problem, we similarly need to define a generalized problem for our analysis. Below, the notation
\vec{U}\sim\mathcal{D}^{\otimes m}
means
\vec{U}=\{U^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
where
U^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{D}
are i.i.d. random variables.

Definition 8
.

A quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
on input
\vec{U}\sim\mathcal{D}^{\otimes m}
is said to solve
b
-
\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
if it outputs
\vec{z}=\{z^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
such that

\underset{\vec{U}\sim\mathcal{D}^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\vec{z}\sim\mathcal{A}^{\vec{U}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i}p_{U^{(i)}}(z^{(i)})\right]\right]\geq\frac{bm}{N}.

(III.5)

Informally, the result we are after should be similar to Theorem
7
with: explicit constants;
\varepsilon^{\prime}
and the overall entropy scaled by a factor of
m
; and improved correction terms. To proceed with this generalization, we follow the analysis of Section 7 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
closely. Some key results use different techniques in order to obtain improved correction terms. The high level idea is as follows. We first show that any oracle access to a Haar random unitary can be simulated by an algorithm that is given
k
bitstrings independently sampled from the measurement probability distribution corresponding to that unitary. Specifically, the quantum state from the oracle access algorithm is
O(1/\sqrt{k})
-close to the quantum state from the algorithm given the bitstrings. We then show that for any algorithm only given samples and no other information about the Haar random unitary, it must output one of the given bitstrings with sufficient probability to do well on
\mathrm{XHOG}
. Since the input samples have high entropy and the output is likely one of the samples, the output must have entropy. Finally, since the oracle access algorithm output quantum state is close to the sample access algorithm output quantum state, the quantum state of the oracle access algorithm must also have entropy.

Unfortunately, even then, the above theory does not result in a good entropy guarantee. Specifically, ignoring the
O(1)
term, assuming
T=1
,
b=2
, and optimizing over
k
, the entropy guarantee is zero for all systems under 70 qubits, and the entropy rate is less than
0.32
up to 100 qubits.

As a result, we seek to improve the entropy guarantee of the theory under the query-access model. Specifically, we can improve the correction term cubic in
k
to quadratic, and improve the term
-2\log_{2}k
to
-\log_{2}k
. We prove the improvement to
-\log_{2}k
against classical side information for all
m
. We also provide a proof against quantum side information for
m=1
, which we believe to be easily generalizable to all
m
despite cumbersome notations.

Additionally, we prove an even more aggressive improvement from
-2\log_{2}k
to no
\log_{2}k
penalty in the special case of
m=1
and
T=1
. Although much more specialized, we believe this is a reasonable adversary model. First, our experimentally implemented protocol is in the
m=1
case. Additionally, only trapped ion devices are capable of executing the challenge circuits at such a high fidelity observed experimentally, and we know that no quantum device today is capable of executing the quantum circuit more than once within the latency window. We additionally believe that generalization to all
m
and
T
should be possible, otherwise an unnatural jump in entropy penalty is present when increasing
T
from 1 to 2.

III.1
Simulation of Haar random unitaries given sample access

We first formally define what we mean by oracle access to a Haar random unitary.

Definition 9
.

For Haar random
U
, we say an efficient device that acts on system
D
is given oracle access to
U
if it can apply
U^{\prime}
to any subset of its system
D
of the same size, where
U^{\prime}=VUW
,
V
is a random phase unitary
\sum_{z}e^{i\theta_{z}}|z\rangle\langle z|
,
|z\rangle
are computational basis states,
\theta_{z}
are independently sampled from
[0,2\pi)
, and
W
is a Haar random unitary on the subspace
\{|x\rangle:x\in\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{0^{n}\}\}
.

This restriction is natural since the client only cares about the bitstring probability amplitudes corresponding to the state prepared acting on the
|0\rangle
state, which is unchanged by applying
W
and
V
. The client can ‘mask’ the original circuit
U
by applying
W
and
V
and the adversary would only have blackbox access to
U^{\prime}
. We can generalize this notion to a batched setting.

Definition 10
.

For
\vec{U}=\{U^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
such that
U^{(i)}
are independent and Haar random, we say an efficient device is given oracle access to
\vec{U}
if it is given oracle access to
U^{(i)}
for all
i\in[m]
.

Under this oracle access model, entropy conditioned on the challenge circuits is the same as entropy conditioned on the corresponding bitstring probability distributions due to the random phase symmetrization and the random unitary
W
symmetrization of the oracle access model.

Theorem 11

(Generalization of Theorem 7.1 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

For
\vec{U}=\{U^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
such that
U^{(i)}
are independent and Haar random, any device
\mathcal{A}
taking on input system
D
of a bipartite state
\rho_{DE}
and outputs a classical quantum state
\psi
given oracle access to
\vec{U}
must have
H(\vec{Z}|\vec{U}E)\psi=H(\vec{Z}|\vec{P}E)_{\psi}
, where
\vec{P}=\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
and
P^{(i)}(z)=|\langle z|U^{(i)}|0\rangle|^{2}
is distributed according to the Dirichlet distribution
\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})
.

Proof.

Using the same arguments as in Theorem 7.1 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, for every distribution
P^{(i)}
, one can simulate a query to
U^{(i)}
using one query to
U^{P^{(i)}}
, where
U^{P^{(i)}}
is a ‘representative’ in the set of all unitaries
U
such that
|\langle z|U|0^{n}\rangle|^{2}=P^{(i)}(z)
. Denote
\vec{U}^{\vec{P}}=\{U^{P^{(i)}}\}_{i\in[m]}
. Therefore, the quantum state given oracle access to
\vec{U}
is the same as the quantum state given oracle access to
\vec{U}^{\vec{P}}
, meaning
\psi^{\vec{U}}=\psi^{\vec{U}^{\vec{P}}}
. Since
\vec{U}^{\vec{P}}
only depends on
\vec{P}
, we can write
\psi^{\vec{U}^{\vec{P}}}=\sigma^{\vec{P}}
. Since the output state of
\mathcal{A}
only depends on
\vec{P}
, the theorem follows.
∎

We now proceed with the main proof. For any
n
-qubit state
|\psi\rangle
, we define several objects in the same way as Section 7.1.1 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, which we reproduce for completeness. Let
C^{\psi}
be a random unitary such that
C^{\psi}|0\rangle=|\psi\rangle
and
C^{\psi}
is Haar random for the subspace orthogonal to
|0\rangle
. We would like to show that accessing such a unitary can be simulated by making queries to the so-called state preparation oracle. Unlike
C^{\psi}
, the state preparation oracle must not contain any additional information other than
|\psi\rangle
.

We first fix a canonical state
|\bot\rangle
orthogonal to all
n
-qubit states, which one can obtain by extending the Hilbert space such as
|\bot\rangle=|0^{n}\rangle|0\rangle
(see footnote 4 of Ref.
Kretschmer 2021a
). Define an oracle
\mathcal{O}^{\psi}
that is a reflection about
|\psi_{\bot}\rangle=\frac{|\psi\rangle-|\bot\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}
, which means
\mathcal{O}^{\psi}|\psi\rangle=|\bot\rangle,\mathcal{O}^{\psi}|\bot\rangle=|\psi\rangle
, and
\mathcal{O}^{\psi}|\phi\rangle=|\phi\rangle
for any
|\phi\rangle
orthogonal to the span of
\left\{|\psi\rangle,|\bot\rangle\right\}
.

Definition 12
.

A
(T,m)
-query algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\vec{\mathcal{O}}}
, where
\vec{\mathcal{O}}=\{\mathcal{O}^{(i)}\}
, is an algorithm that makes at most
T
queries to
\mathcal{O}^{(i)}
for all
i\in[m]
.

We now show that oracle access to the circuits can be approximated by querying the state preparation oracle.

Lemma 13

(Generalization of Theorem 7.2 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

For every set of
m
quantum states
\{|\psi^{(i)}\rangle\}_{i\in[m]}
, denote
\vec{C}=\{C^{\psi^{(i)}}\}_{i\in[m]}
and
\vec{\mathcal{O}}=\{\mathcal{O}^{\psi^{(i)}}\}
. Every
(T,m)
-query algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\vec{C}}
can be approximated by a
(2T,m)
-query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{B}^{\vec{\mathcal{O}}}
such that

\left\|\mathcal{B}^{\vec{\mathcal{O}}}-\underset{\vec{C}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{\vec{C}}\right]\right\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{4Tm}{2^{n/2}}.

(III.6)

Proof.

Define
\mathcal{C}^{\psi}(\rho)=C^{\psi}\rho(C^{\psi})^{\dagger}
. As discussed in the proof of Theorem 7.2 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, for any state
|\psi^{\bot}\rangle
orthogonal to
|\psi\rangle
, one can replace a call to a random
\mathcal{C}^{\psi}
with
\Phi^{\psi}
defined as (fixing a typo in Eq. 175 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
),

\displaystyle\Phi^{\psi}(\rho)=\underset{|\varphi\rangle\sim\mathrm{Haar(2^{n})}}{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{O}^{\psi}\mathcal{O}^{\varphi}\mathcal{O}^{\psi}V^{\varphi}W\rho(\mathcal{O}^{\psi}\mathcal{O}^{\varphi}\mathcal{O}^{\psi}V^{\varphi}W)^{\dagger}],

(III.7)

satisfying
\|\Phi^{\psi}-\mathcal{C}^{\psi}\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}
as stated in Eq. 178. Also, from the definition of
\Phi^{\psi}
in Eq. 175 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, each
\mathcal{O}^{\psi^{(i)}}
is queried
2T
times. For
\vec{\Phi}=\{\Phi^{\psi^{(i)}}\}_{i\in[m]}
, we can choose the algorithm
\mathcal{B}^{\vec{\mathcal{O}}}=\mathcal{B}^{\vec{\Phi}}
. This is a
(2T,m)
-query algorithm. The total error bound of the lemma follows from the triangle inequality.

∎

We now show that the state preparation oracle can be approximated given some resource state that depends on the Haar random state. We reproduce the definition of the resource state considered in Theorem 7.4 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
here:

|R^{\psi}\rangle=|\psi\rangle^{\otimes\ell}\otimes\bigotimes_{i\in[k]}\left(\cos\left(\frac{i\pi}{2k}\right)|\psi\rangle+\sin\left(\frac{i\pi}{2k}\right)|\bot\rangle\right).

(III.8)

Lemma 14

(Generalization of Corollary 7.4 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

For any state
|\psi^{(i)}\rangle
, define
|R^{\psi^{(i)}}\rangle
as Eq.
III.8
, and define
|\vec{R}\rangle=\bigotimes_{i\in[m]}|R^{\psi^{(i)}}\rangle
. Let
\mathcal{O}
be an oracle and
\rho
be a quantum state. For every algorithm
\mathcal{B}
that takes
\rho
as input and makes
(T,m)
-query to
\vec{O}
, there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{G}
that on input
\rho,|\vec{R}\rangle
makes the same number of queries to
\mathcal{O}
as
\mathcal{B}
such that

\left\|\mathcal{B}^{\vec{\mathcal{O}},\mathcal{O}}(\rho)-\mathcal{G}^{\mathcal{O}}(\rho,|\vec{R}\rangle)\right\|_{1}\leq Tm\left(\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k}\right)^{k}\right)}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}\right).

(III.9)

Proof.

Eq. 11 and 12 of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
are true for each
|\psi^{(i)}\rangle
(in the notations of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
,
|\Psi\rangle
). For Lemma 41 of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
, each invocation of
\mathcal{O}_{\Psi}
is replaced with
U_{\Psi}
, which are
\varepsilon_{n}
close to each other due to Eq. 11 of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
. This allows us to write down a
(T,m)
-query version of Lemma 41 of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
with trace distance
Tm\sqrt{2(1-(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k})^{k})}
(or
q_{\Psi}\sqrt{2(1-(\cos\frac{\pi}{2n})^{n})}
in the notations of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
, where the expression is taken from the paragraph above Eq. 11 and
q_{\Psi}
comes from the fact that it makes
q_{\Psi}
queries).

For Lemma 42 of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
, exactly the same arguments apply from Eq. 12 of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
. This allows us to write down a
(T,m)
-query version of Lemma 42 of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
with trace distance
2Tm/\sqrt{\ell+1}
(or
2q_{\rm Ref}/\sqrt{m+1}
in the notations of Ref.
Ambainis et al. 2014
). Combining the
(T,m)
-query version of Lemma 41 and 42 concludes the proof.
∎

This now allows us to establish a generalization of Corollary 7.6 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, which states that oracle access to the Haar random circuit can be approximated by accessing the resource state. It turns out that the inclusion of the
\theta
parameter throughout the proof of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
is unnecessary, so we omit it in further discussions.

Corollary 15

(Generalization of Corollary 7.6 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
but without
\theta
)
.

For quantum state
\{|\psi^{(i)}\rangle\}_{i\in[m]}
and every quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
making
(T,m)
-queries to
\vec{C}
, there exists a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{G}
given access to
|\vec{R}\rangle
such that

\left\|\underset{\vec{C}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{\vec{C}}\right]-\mathcal{G}\left(|\vec{R}\rangle\langle\vec{R}|\right)\right\|_{\diamond}\leq Tm\left(\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k}\right)^{k}\right)}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}\right).

(III.10)

Proof.

The proof follows the same idea as Corollary 7.6 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
by combining Lemma
13
and Lemma
14
.
∎

We now show that access to resource states can be simulated by access to samples drawn from
|\psi\rangle
.
We reproduce some definitions from
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
. Consider
P=(P_{0},\dots,P_{N-1})\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})
, a random state
|\psi\rangle=W|P\rangle
, where
|P\rangle=(\sqrt{P_{0}},\dots,\sqrt{P_{N-1}})
and
W
is a random diagonal phase matrix. We can now define the resource state

|R^{P,W}\rangle=(W|P\rangle)^{\otimes\ell}\otimes\bigotimes_{i\in[k]}\left(\cos\left(\frac{i\pi}{2k}\right)W|P\rangle+\sin\left(\frac{i\pi}{2k}\right)|\bot\rangle\right).

(III.11)

Lemma 16

(Lemma 7.7 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, rephrased from Lemma 15 of Ref.
Kretschmer 2021a
)
.

There is an algorithm which prepares

\sigma^{P}=\underset{W}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|R^{P,W}\rangle\langle R^{P,W}|\right]

(III.12)

by measuring
(k+\ell)
copies of
|\psi\rangle
in the standard basis. Here,
|\psi\rangle
is any state with probability distribution
P
.

We note that this Lemma follows when we consider the state
|R\rangle
of Lemma 15 of Ref.
Kretschmer 2021a
with
j\in[k+\ell]
and
\alpha_{j}=1,\beta_{j}=0
for
j\in[\ell]
.

We now define
\vec{W}=\{W^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
, where
W^{(i)}
are independent random diagonal phase matrices. Similarly, we define
\vec{P}=\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
, and also use the notation
\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})^{\otimes m}
to denote
P^{(i)}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})
for all
i\in[m]
. We now define the resource state

|R^{\vec{P},\vec{W}}\rangle=\bigotimes_{i\in[m]}|R^{P^{(i)},W^{(i)}}\rangle.

(III.13)

Lemma 17

(Generalization of Lemma
16
)
.

There is an algorithm which prepares

\sigma^{\vec{P}}=\underset{\vec{W}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|R^{\vec{P},\vec{W}}\rangle\langle R^{\vec{P},\vec{W}}|\right]

(III.14)

by measuring
(k+\ell)
copies of
|\psi^{(i)}\rangle
for every
i\in[m]
. Here,
|\psi^{i}\rangle
is any state with probability distribution
P^{(i)}
.

Proof.

It can be shown that
\sigma^{\vec{P}}=\bigotimes_{i\in[m]}\sigma^{P^{(i)}}
, hence the proof follows from Lemma
16
.
∎

We now show that any algorithm given oracle access to Haar random circuits can be simulated by an algorithm with sample access. We consider random circuits given distribution
P
in the same way as
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
. For any distribution
P
, let
C^{P}=WVC^{\prime}
, where
W
is a random diagonal phase matrix,
V|0\rangle=|P\rangle
, and
C^{\prime}
is Haar random on the subspace orthogonal to
|0\rangle
. Similarly,
\vec{C}^{\vec{P}}=\{C^{P^{(i)}}\}_{i\in[m]}
, where each
C^{P^{(i)}}
are independently and randomly sampled as above.

For
\vec{z}=\{z^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
, we use the following notation
\vec{z}\sim\vec{P}
to denote
z^{(i)}\sim P^{(i)}
for all
i\in[m]
.

Theorem 18

(Generalization of Theorem 7.8 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

For any
\vec{P}
and any algorithm
\mathcal{A}
making
(T,m)
-query to
\vec{C}^{\vec{P}}
, there exists a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{F}
given
(k+\ell)
samples drawn from
P^{(i)}
for all
i\in[m]
such that

\|\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\vec{P}}-\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\vec{P}}\|_{\diamond}\leq Tm\left(\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k}\right)^{k}\right)}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}\right),

(III.15)

where
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\vec{P}}=\mathbb{E}_{\vec{C}^{\vec{P}}}[\mathcal{A}^{\vec{C}^{\vec{P}}}]
and
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\vec{P}}(\rho)=\mathbb{E}_{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}\sim\vec{P}}[\mathcal{F}(\rho,\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})]
.

Proof.

We closely mirror the arguments presented in Theorem 7.8 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
. Given
P
, sampling random
C^{P}
is equivalent to sampling random diagonal phase matrix
W
to produce
|\psi\rangle=W|P\rangle
, and then sample random circuit
C^{\psi}
such that
C^{\psi}|0\rangle=|\psi\rangle
and
C^{\psi}
is Haar random for the subspace orthogonal to
|0\rangle
, as described after Theorem
11
. Similarly, given
m
distributions
\vec{P}
, sampling random
\vec{C}^{\vec{P}}
is equivalent to sampling
m
random diagonal phase matrices
\vec{W}
, producing
m
states
\{|\psi^{(i)}\rangle=W^{(i)}|P^{(i)}\rangle\}
, and then sample
m
random circuits
\vec{C}=\{C^{\psi^{(i)}}\}
in the same way as above. Therefore,

\underset{\vec{C}^{\vec{P}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{\vec{C}^{\vec{P}}}\right]=\underset{\vec{W},\vec{C}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{\vec{C}}\right].

(III.16)

Let the quantum process that takes samples to prepare
\sigma^{\vec{P}}
in Lemma
17
be
\Phi
. By the same argument as
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
,

\underset{\vec{W}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{G}\left(|R^{\vec{P},\vec{W}}\rangle\langle R^{\vec{P},\vec{W}}|\right)\right]=\underset{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}\sim\vec{P}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{G}\circ\Phi(\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})\right].

(III.17)

With
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{G}\circ\Phi
, triangle inequality and Corollary
15
,

\displaystyle\left\|\underset{\vec{C}^{\vec{P}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{\vec{C}^{\vec{P}}}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}\sim\vec{P}}[\mathcal{F}(\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})]\right\|_{\diamond}\leq

\displaystyle\underset{\vec{W}}{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\underset{\vec{C}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{\vec{C}}\right]-\mathcal{G}\left(|R^{\vec{P},\vec{W}}\rangle\langle R^{\vec{P},\vec{W}}|\right)\right\|_{\diamond}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle Tm\left(\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k}\right)^{k}\right)}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}\right).

(III.18)

∎

III.2
A simplified device

We now show that sample access algorithms must provide entropy if it passes solves
\mathrm{MXHOG}
. Below, we provide a generalization of Theorem 7.10 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, where we improve the correction from
k^{3}
to
k^{2}
. This theorem shows that solving
\mathrm{MXHOG}
implies some constant probability of returning one of the input samples.

Theorem 19

(Generalization of Theorem 7.10 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

For a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{F}
given
(k+\ell)
samples draw from
P^{(i)}
for all
i\in[m]
, if
n\geq 50
,
k\leq N^{1/2}
, and

\underset{\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{z}\sim\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\vec{P}}(\rho)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]\geq m\frac{2-\varepsilon}{N+k},

(III.19)

where
\vec{P}=\{P^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
and
\vec{z}=\{z^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
, then with probability greater than
1-6m/N
,

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}\Pr\left[z^{(i)}\in\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k]}\right]\geq 1-\varepsilon-1.001\times\frac{16k^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N},

(III.20)

where
\vec{z}_{j}=\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
, and the probability is over choices of
\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})^{\otimes m},\vec{z}_{1},\dots\vec{z}_{k}\sim\vec{P}
, and
\vec{z}=\{z^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}\sim\mathcal{F}(\rho,\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})
.

Proof.

For each set of samples
\{z_{1}^{(i)},\dots,z_{k}^{(i)}\}
obtained from distribution
P^{(i)}
, let
f^{(i)}=\{f_{0}^{(i)},\dots,f_{N-1}^{(i)}\}
be the frequency vector. Denote
\vec{f}=\{f^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}
, the output of
\mathcal{F}(\rho,\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})
can be described by a distribution
Q(\vec{f})
that only depends on
\vec{f}
. Due to Lemma 7.9 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
,
f^{(i)}
is distributed according to the uniform distribution of all frequency vectors that has
N
elements and sums to
k
, which we denote as
f^{(i)}\sim\Phi(N,k)
. We use the notation
\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m}
to denote the fact that this holds for all
i\in[m]
.

Upon observing
f^{(i)}
, the posterior distribution of
P^{(i)}
follows
\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N}+f^{(i)})
, as described by Eq. 200 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
. For each
\vec{f}
such that
\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=j
for some
i\in[m]
, the contribution of the
i
th circuit to the
\mathrm{MXHOG}
score is given by

\underset{\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{P^{(i)}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N}+f^{(i)})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]=\underset{\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{f_{z^{(i)}}^{(i)}+1}{N+k}\right]\leq\frac{1}{N+k}\left(1+j\cdot\underset{\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\Pr}\left[f_{z^{(i)}}^{(i)}>0\right]\right),

(III.21)

where the first equality uses the property of the Dirichlet distribution in the same way as Eq. 200 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, and the inequality follows from
\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=j\rightarrow\forall z,f_{z}^{(i)}\leq j
.

Denote the event that
\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=j
as
\Omega^{(i)}_{j}
, and the event
\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}\geq j
as
\Omega^{(i)+}_{j}
. We now upper bound
\Pr\left[\Omega^{(i)+}_{j}\right]
, which is the probability that at least one of the
N
possible bitstrings are sampled at least
j
times. Given any bitstring
s
, the probability that a specific choice of a set of
j
out of
k
samples are
s
(other samples not in the set are allowed to be
s
) is
P(s)^{j}\leq(\max_{z}P(z))^{j}
. Since there are
\binom{k}{j}
choices of
j
samples, by the union bound, the probability that any given bitstring is sampled at least
j
times is upper bounded by
\binom{k}{j}\left(\max_{z}P(z)\right)^{j}
. By Lemma 3.14 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, with probability
1-6/N
over
P\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})
, we have
\max_{z}P(z)\leq 4\frac{\ln N}{N}=p_{\rm max}
. Therefore, with probability greater than
1-6m/N
, for all
i\in[m]
, the probability that any given bitstring is sampled at least
j
times is at most
\binom{k}{j}\left(4\frac{\ln N}{N}\right)^{j}
. There are
N
possible bitstrings, and by the union bound,

\Pr\left[\Omega^{(i)+}_{j}\right]\leq N\cdot\binom{k}{j}\cdot\left(4\frac{\ln N}{N}\right)^{j}\leq\frac{(4k\ln N)^{j}}{j}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{j-1}},

(III.22)

where we used the fact that

\binom{k}{j}=\frac{k\times(k-1)\times\dots\times(k-j+1)}{j\times(j-1)\times\dots\times 1}\leq\frac{k^{j}}{j}.

(III.23)

The score of
\mathcal{F}
in expectation is

\displaystyle\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N}+\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[m]}\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N}+\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N+k}\sum_{i\in[m]}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\Pr\left[\Omega^{(i)}_{j}\right]\left(1+j\cdot\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m},\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\Pr}\left[f^{(i)}_{z^{(i)}}>0\Big|\Omega^{(i)}_{j}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N+k}\left\{m+\sum_{i\in[m]}\left(\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m},\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\Pr}\left[f^{(i)}_{z^{(i)}}>0\right]+\sum_{j=2}^{k}j\cdot\Pr\left[\Omega^{(i)+}_{j}\right]\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N+k}\left\{m+\frac{m16k^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(4k\ln N)^{j}}{N^{j}}+\sum_{i\in[m]}\left(\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m},\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\Pr}\left[f^{(i)}_{z^{(i)}}>0\right]\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N+k}\left\{m+1.001\times\frac{m16k^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}+\sum_{i\in[m]}\left(\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m},\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\Pr}\left[f^{(i)}_{z^{(i)}}>0\right]\right)\right\},

(III.24)

where the first inequality uses Eq.
III.21
, the second inequality uses the fact that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\Omega^{(i)}_{1}\right]\Pr\left[f^{(i)}_{z^{(i)}}>0\Big|\Omega^{(i)}_{1}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\Pr\left[f^{(i)}_{z^{(i)}}>0\right]

(III.25)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\Omega^{(i)}_{j}\right]\Pr\left[f^{(i)}_{z^{(i)}}>0\Big|\Omega^{(i)}_{j}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\Omega^{(i)}_{j}\right]\leq\Pr\left[\Omega^{(i)+}_{j}\right],

(III.26)

where we omit
\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m},\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})
in the notation
\Pr
, the third inequality uses Eq.
III.22
, and the last inequality uses the fact that

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(4k\ln N)^{j}}{N^{j}}=\frac{1}{1-(4k\ln N)/N}\leq 1.001

(III.27)

for
n\geq 50
and
k\leq N^{1/2}
.

Thus if the score is at least
m\frac{2-\varepsilon}{N+k}
,

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m}\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\Pr}\left[f_{z^{(i)}}^{(i)}>0\right]\geq 1-\varepsilon-1.001\times\frac{16k^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}.

(III.28)

∎

Below, we show that algorithms returning one of the input samples must have entropy by proving a generalization of Theorem 7.11 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
to
\mathrm{MXHOG}
, where the proof techniques differ slightly to improve the
2\log_{2}k
factor to
\log_{2}k
.

Theorem 20

(Generalization of Theorem 7.11 and Corollary 7.12 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

For distributions
\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})^{\otimes m}
, let
\mathcal{F}
be any algorithm that produce an output register
\vec{Z}=Z^{(1)}\dots Z^{(m)}
given access to
\vec{z}_{1},\dots\vec{z}_{k}\sim\vec{P}
over register
\vec{Z}_{1}\dots\vec{Z}_{k}
and the first subsystem of any bipartition state
\rho_{DE}
. Denote
\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\vec{P}}(\rho)=\mathbb{E}_{\vec{z}_{1},\dots\vec{z}_{k}\sim\vec{P}}[\mathcal{F}(\rho,\vec{z}_{1},\dots\vec{z}_{k})]
. If
n\geq 50
,
k\leq N^{-1/2}
,
m,n\leq 10^{6}
,
\forall i\in[m],\max_{z}P^{(i)}(z)\leq p_{\rm max}=4\ln N/N
, and

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}\underset{\vec{z}_{1},\dots\vec{z}_{k}\sim\vec{P},\vec{z}\sim\mathcal{F}(\rho,\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})}{\Pr}\left[z^{(i)}\in\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k]}\right]\geq 1-\delta,

(III.29)

then, conditioned on classical side information
E
,

H(\vec{Z}|\vec{P}E)_{\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\vec{P}}(\rho)}\geq m\left[n(1-\delta)-\log_{2}n-\log_{2}k-3\right].

(III.30)

Proof.

First, consider a fixed set of distributions
\vec{P}
and evaluate the entropy conditioned on
E
. Define the classical-quantum state

\psi_{\vec{Z}_{1}\dots\vec{Z}_{k}\vec{Z}E}=\sum_{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{1})\dots\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{k})|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}\rangle\langle\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}|_{\vec{Z}_{1}\dots\vec{Z}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{F}(\rho,\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})_{\vec{Z}E},

(III.31)

where
\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{j})=\prod_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)}_{j})
.
For the time being, let us ignore
E
, and the state can be stated in terms of some conditional distribution
Q(\cdot|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})
,

\psi_{\vec{Z}_{1}\dots\vec{Z}_{k}\vec{Z}}=\sum_{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{1})\dots\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{k})|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}\rangle\langle\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}|_{\vec{Z}_{1}\dots\vec{Z}_{k}}\otimes\sum_{\vec{z}}Q(\vec{z}|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})|\vec{z}\rangle\langle\vec{z}|.

(III.32)

We decompose each distribution

Q(\vec{z}|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})=\sum_{\vec{s}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}Q_{\vec{s}}(\vec{z}|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}),

(III.33)

where for
\vec{s}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}
and
\vec{z}=\{z^{(i)}\}_{i\in[m]}\in\mathrm{support}(Q_{\vec{s}}(\cdot|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}))
, we have
z^{(i)}\in\{z_{1}^{(i)},\dots,z_{k}^{(i)}\}
for all
i\in[m]
such that
s_{i}=1
and
z^{(i)}\notin\{z_{1}^{(i)},\dots,z_{k}^{(i)}\}
for all
i\in[m]
such that
s_{i}=0
. Let us formally denote this condition as

\displaystyle\vec{z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vec{s}}}{{\in}}\{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall i\in[m],(s_{i}=1\wedge z^{(i)}\in\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k]})\vee(s_{i}=0\wedge z^{(i)}\notin\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k]}).

(III.34)

Define the following probabilities:

\displaystyle p_{\vec{s},\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\vec{z}}Q_{\vec{s}}(\vec{z}|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})

(III.35)

\displaystyle p_{\vec{s}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}p_{\vec{s},\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{1})\dots\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{k})

(III.36)

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{\vec{s}}(\vec{z}|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})

\displaystyle=Q_{\vec{s}}(\vec{z}|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})/p_{\vec{s},\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}},

(III.37)

we can define normalized states

\psi_{\vec{s}}=\frac{1}{p_{\vec{s}}}\sum_{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}p_{\vec{s},\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{1})\dots\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{k})|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}\rangle\langle\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}|_{\vec{Z}_{1}\dots\vec{Z}_{k}}\otimes\sum_{\vec{z}}\bar{Q}_{\vec{s}}(\vec{z}|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})|\vec{z}\rangle\langle\vec{z}|,

(III.38)

and therefore
\psi=\sum_{\vec{s}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}p_{\vec{s}}\psi_{\vec{s}}
.

Consider
\sigma_{\vec{s}}=\mathrm{tr}_{\vec{Z}_{1}\dots\vec{Z}_{k}}(\psi_{\vec{s}})
. The operator
\sigma_{\vec{s}}
requires that
\vec{z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vec{s}}}{{\in}}\{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}\}
as described earlier.

\displaystyle\langle\vec{z}|\sigma_{\vec{s}}|\vec{z}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p_{\vec{s}}}\sum_{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}p_{\vec{s},\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{1})\dots\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{k})\bar{Q}_{\vec{s}}(\vec{z}|\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p_{\vec{s}}}\sum_{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}p_{\vec{s},\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{1})\dots\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{k})\mathbb{I}[\vec{z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vec{s}}}{{\in}}\{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}\}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p_{\vec{s}}}\sum_{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}p_{\vec{s},\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{1})\dots\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{k})\prod_{i\in[m]:s_{i}=1}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathbb{I}[z^{(i)}=z^{(i)}_{j}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p_{\vec{s}}}\prod_{i\in[m]:s_{i}=1}\sum_{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}p_{\vec{s},\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{1})\dots\vec{P}(\vec{z}_{k})\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathbb{I}[z^{(i)}=z^{(i)}_{j}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p_{\vec{s}}}\prod_{i\in[m]:s_{i}=1}\sum_{j\in[k]}p^{(ij)}_{z^{(i)}}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)}),

(III.39)

where

\displaystyle p^{(ij)}_{z^{(i)}}\equiv\underset{\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{j-1},\vec{z}_{j+1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{z^{(1)}_{j},\dots,z^{(m-1)}_{j},z^{(m+1)}_{j},\dots,z^{(m)}_{j}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[p_{\vec{s},\vec{z_{1}},\dots,\vec{z}_{j}=\{z^{(1)}_{j},\dots,z^{(i-1)}_{j},z^{(i)},z^{(i+1)}_{j},\dots,z^{(m)}_{j}\},\dots,\vec{z}_{k}}\right]\right].

(III.40)

Further, by definition,
p^{(ij)}_{z^{(i)}}\in[0,1]
, and thus

\displaystyle\langle\vec{z}|\sigma_{\vec{s}}|\vec{z}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p_{\vec{s}}}\prod_{i\in[m]:s_{i}=1}k\max_{z}P^{(i)}(z)\leq\frac{1}{p_{\vec{s}}}(kp_{\rm max})^{w(\vec{s})}

(III.41)

\displaystyle H(\vec{Z})_{\sigma_{\vec{s}}}

\displaystyle\geq H_{\rm min}(\vec{Z})_{\sigma_{\vec{s}}}\geq w(\vec{s})\left(\log_{2}\frac{1}{p_{\rm max}}-\log_{2}k\right)-\log_{2}\frac{1}{p_{\rm s}}\geq w(\vec{s})\left(n-\log_{2}n-2-\log_{2}k\right)-\log_{2}\frac{1}{p_{\vec{s}}},

(III.42)

where
w(\vec{s})
is the Hamming weight of
\vec{s}
, and we used the fact that
\log_{2}\frac{1}{p_{\rm min}}\geq-\log_{2}\frac{4\ln N}{N}=n-\log_{2}n-\log_{2}\ln 2-2\geq n-\log_{2}n-2
.

Back to the general state unconditioned on any
\vec{s}
,

\displaystyle H(\vec{Z})_{\psi}

\displaystyle\geq H(\vec{Z}|\vec{s})_{\psi}=\sum_{\vec{s}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}p_{\vec{s}}H(\vec{Z})_{\sigma_{\vec{s}}}

\displaystyle=(n-\log_{2}n-2-\log_{2}k)\sum_{\vec{s}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}p_{\vec{s}}w(\vec{s})+\sum_{\vec{s}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}p_{\vec{s}}\log_{2}p_{\vec{s}}

\displaystyle=(n-\log_{2}n-2-\log_{2}k)\sum_{i\in[m]}\underset{\vec{z}_{1},\dots\vec{z}_{k}\sim\vec{P},\vec{z}\sim\mathcal{F}(\rho,\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{k})}{\Pr}\left[z^{(i)}\in\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k]}\right]-H(\vec{s})_{\psi}

\displaystyle\geq m\left[n(1-\delta)-\log_{2}n-\log_{2}k-3\right],

(III.43)

where the last line uses the fact that the sum in the previous line is
1-\delta
. However, the theorem demands entropy conditioned on
E
. For classical side information, we are done since any classical side information can be absorbed into the strategy of the adversary to minimize entropy. Further, since the proof is for fixed
\vec{P}
, conditioning on
\vec{P}
gives the same bound.
∎

We note that the above theorem with the improvement is conditioned on classical side information. For randomness amplification, since the quantum device is assumed to be the adversary, the quantum bits themselves do not remain private, only unpredictable upon initial snapshot of information of the adversary. In this case, entropy conditioned on quantum side information is unnecessary. This is similarly the case for other use cases where the quantum randomness will be public such as multi-party protocols.

We can obtain a version with quantum side information that also improves the entropy penalty from
2\log_{2}k
to
\log_{2}k
. We do not generalize to the multiple input setting due to cumbersome notation. We defer the proof to Subsection
VIII.1

Combining Theorem
19
and Theorem
20
yields the following corollary, which states that sample access algorithms solving
\mathrm{MXHOG}
must have entropy.

Corollary 21

(Generalization of Corollary 7.13 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be any device given access to
k
samples from each distribution
P^{(i)}=P_{C^{(i)}}
in
\vec{P}
for
C^{(i)}\sim\mathrm{Haar}(N)
. If
n\geq 50
,
k\leq N^{-1/2}
,
m,n\leq 10^{6}
, and
\mathcal{F}
solves
(1+\delta)-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
, then

H(\vec{Z}|\vec{P}E)_{\Psi}\geq m\left[\left(\delta-1.001\times\frac{16k^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}\right)n-\log_{2}n-\log_{2}k-3\right].

(III.44)

Proof.

We first use Theorem
19
. Solving
(1+\delta)-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
requires
\frac{1+\delta}{N}=\frac{2-\varepsilon}{N+k}\rightarrow\varepsilon\leq 1-\delta+k/N
, which implies the average probability that the output sample is in the set of
k
samples is at least
\delta-k/N-1.001\times 16k^{2}\ln^{2}N/N
. For the parameters of interest,
k/N
can be safely absorbed into
1.001\times 16k^{2}\ln^{2}N/N
since the constant
1.001
is loose. Combining with Theorem
20
completes the proof. The
1-6m/N
probability in Theorem
19
results in a small proportional decrease in the entropy. For
m<10^{6}
and
n>50
, this results in a correction smaller than
10^{-8}m
and can be absorbed into constants of correction terms since these corrections are loose.
∎

III.3
The analysis of a general device

We now show that algorithms with oracle access to Haar random circuits and solve
\mathrm{MXHOG}
must have entropy. We first provide a generalization of Lemma 3.13 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
while fixing some technical errors.

Lemma 22

(Generalization of Lemma 3.13 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

\underset{\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{1}{m}\max_{\vec{z}}\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]\leq\frac{2}{N}(n\ln 2+3),

(III.45)

where
P^{(i)}(z)
is the probability of sampling
z
from
P^{(i)}
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\underset{P\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max_{z}P(z)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{Q\sim\Gamma(1,1)^{N}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\max Q}{\bar{Q}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{Q\sim\Gamma(1,1)^{N}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\max Q}{\bar{Q}}\Big|\bar{Q}\geq N/2\right]\Pr[\bar{Q}\geq N/2]+\underset{Q\sim\Gamma(1,1)^{N}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\max Q}{\bar{Q}}\Big|\bar{Q}<N/2\right]\Pr[\bar{Q}<N/2]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{N}\underset{Q\sim\Gamma(1,1)^{N}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max Q\Big|\bar{Q}\geq N/2\right]\Pr[\bar{Q}\geq N/2]+\Pr[\bar{Q}<N/2]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{N}\underset{Q\sim\Gamma(1,1)^{N}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max Q\right]+\Pr[\bar{Q}<N/2]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{N}(\ln N+1)+\frac{4}{N}=\frac{2}{N}(n\ln 2+3),

(III.46)

where
Q
is a list of
N
independent random variables drawn from
\Gamma(1,1)
,
\bar{Q}
is the sum of all values in
Q
,

\mathbb{E}[\max(Q_{1},\dots,Q_{m})]
is from Eq. 31 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
,
\Pr[\bar{Q}<N/2]
is from Eq. 32 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
,
and second last line uses the fact that

\mathbb{E}[X|A]\Pr[A]=\mathbb{E}[X]-\mathbb{E}[X|\neg A](1-\Pr[A])\rightarrow\mathbb{E}[X|A]\Pr[A]\leq\mathbb{E}[X].

(III.47)

Using the fact that

\frac{1}{m}\max_{\vec{z}}\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}\max_{z^{(i)}}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)}),

(III.48)

we have

\underset{\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max_{\vec{z}}\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]=\underset{\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i\in[m]}\max_{z}P^{(i)}(z)\right]=\sum_{i\in[m]}\underset{P^{(i)}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max_{z}P^{(i)}(z)\right]\leq\frac{2m}{N}(n\ln 2+3),

(III.49)

where the last inequality uses Eq.
III.46

∎

Theorem 23

(Generalization of Theorem 7.14 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

For every
(T,m)
-query device
\mathcal{A}
that solves
b-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
, there exists a device
\mathcal{F}
given access to
(k+\ell)
samples for each of the
m
circuits such that
\mathcal{F}
solves
b^{\prime}-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
for

b^{\prime}=b-2m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3)

(III.50)

and
\varepsilon^{\prime}=T\left(\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k}\right)^{k}\right)}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}\right)
. Similarly, for every
\mathcal{A}
that cannot solve
b-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
, there exists a device
\mathcal{F}
that cannot solve
b^{\prime}-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
for

b^{\prime}=b+2m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3).

(III.51)

Proof.

The argument mirrors that of Theorem 7.14 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
. For every
\mathcal{A}^{\vec{P}}
, by Theorem
18
, there exists
\mathcal{F}^{\vec{P}}
such that

\displaystyle\left|\underset{\vec{z}\sim\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\vec{P}}(\rho)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]-\underset{\vec{z}\sim\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\vec{P}}(\rho)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{m}\sum_{\vec{z}}\left(p_{\mathcal{A}}(\vec{z})-p_{\mathcal{F}}(\vec{z})\right)\cdot\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{m}\left(\max_{\vec{z}}\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right)\sum_{\vec{z}}\left|p_{\mathcal{A}}(\vec{z})-p_{\mathcal{F}}(\vec{z})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{m}\left(\max_{\vec{z}}\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right)\|\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\vec{P}}(\rho)-\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\vec{P}}(\rho)\|_{\mathrm{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq m\varepsilon^{\prime}\frac{1}{m}\max_{\vec{z}}\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)}),

(III.52)

where
\vec{P}(\vec{z})
is the probability of sampling
\vec{z}
from
\vec{P}
. Taking the expectation over
\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})^{\otimes m}
, the upper bound is at most
m\varepsilon^{\prime}\frac{2}{N}(n\ln 2+3)
by Lemma
22
.
∎

Lemma 24

(Lemma 2 of Ref.
Winter 2016
, rephrased)
.

For states
\rho
and
\sigma
on Hilbert space
A\otimes B
, if
\frac{1}{2}\|\rho-\sigma\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon\leq 1
and
A
is classical, then

|S(A|B)_{\rho}-S(A|B)_{\sigma}|\leq\varepsilon\log_{2}|A|+(1+\varepsilon)h\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}\right).

(III.53)

Theorem 25

(Generalization of Theorem 7.15 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be any algorithm making
(T,m)
-queries to
\vec{U}\sim\mathrm{Haar}^{\otimes m}
. If
n\geq 50
,
k\leq N^{-1/2}
,
m,n\leq 10^{6}
, and
\mathcal{A}
solves
(1+\delta)-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
, then

H(\vec{Z}|\vec{U}E)_{\mathcal{A}^{\vec{U}}}\geq m\left[\left(\delta-2m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)-1.001\times\frac{16(k+\ell)^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}\right)n-\log_{2}n-\log_{2}(k+\ell)-3\right]-2

(III.54)

and
\varepsilon^{\prime}=T\left(\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k}\right)^{k}\right)}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}\right)
.

Proof.

We follow similar arguments in
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
. By Theorem
18
, for every
(T,m)
-query
\mathcal{A}
, there exists
\mathcal{F}
given access to
(k+\ell)
samples for each circuit (drawn according the probability distribution induced by the circuit) such that
\|\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{\vec{P}}-\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\vec{P}}\|_{\diamond}\leq m\varepsilon^{\prime}
. Denote the output state of
\bar{\mathcal{F}}
as
\Psi
. Also, by Theorem
23
,
\mathcal{F}
solves
b-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
for
b=1+\delta-2m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3)
. Combining Theorem
23
, Corollary
21
and Theorem
11
, this yields

H(\vec{Z}|\vec{U}E)_{\Psi}\geq m\left[\left(\delta-2m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3)-1.001\times\frac{16(k+\ell)^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}\right)n-\log_{2}n-\log_{2}(k+\ell)-3\right].

(III.55)

This provides a bound on a state
\Psi
close to the actual state of the algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\vec{U}}
. We can now use the continuity bound of entropy in Lemma
24
to bound the entropy of the actual state. The dimension of the first system is
2^{mn}
, so entropy is reduced by
m\varepsilon^{\prime}mn+(1+m\varepsilon^{\prime})h\left(\frac{m\varepsilon^{\prime}}{1+\varepsilon^{\prime}}\right)\leq m\varepsilon^{\prime}mn+2
, which completes the proof.
∎

III.4
Further improvement in the
\log(k+\ell)
entropy penalty

The
\log(k+\ell)
penalty in the entropy is unnatural. Specifically, the oracle is only capable of outputting a single
n
-qubit state, and the
k+\ell
samples are not utilized in the simulation of the oracles in a very exploitative way (e.g. only having a cyclic shift applied to it). It is not actually possible for the adversary to read the bitstrings and select a specific sample to minimize the entropy.

To see this, we sketch the following intuitive argument. Having oracle access to a unitary
V
is equivalent to having access to
fU
, where
f
is a random one-to-one function that maps all bitstrings to all bitstrings and
U=f^{-1}V
. Further, having access to
U
is equivalent to having access to
k+\ell
samples from
p_{U}
, which is equivalent to having access to
k+\ell
samples of
f^{-1}(z_{i})
for
z_{i}\sim p_{V}
. The random function means that the sample access algorithm receives uniformly random bitstrings which will be inversely mapped back to
z_{i}
. The algorithm cannot select the bitstring to output to minimize the entropy, since the unknown function
f
performs the inverse mapping.

Note that in the above argument, it is not the case that the client chooses some
f
and hides from the adversary and provide it with the blackbox access. We are saying that the oracle access to
V
is equivalent to having oracle access to random
f
and use
f
and oracle access to
U
in this specific form.

To sketch out this formally, we consider the special case of
m=1
and
T=1
, which is analytically more tractable. We believe the argument is also applicable to general cases, although the proof would be more involved due to the complexities introduced by correlated random phases applied to the unitaries as well as the fact that the oracles may not be implemented in parallel.

Theorem 26
.

For any
P
and any algorithm
\mathcal{A}
making
(1,1)
-query to
C^{P}
, there exists a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{B}
given one sample drawn from
P
such that

\|\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{P}-\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{P}\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2(1-(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k})^{k})}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}+\frac{2(k+\ell)(k+\ell-1)}{N}+\frac{2k}{N(1-k/N)},

(III.56)

where
\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{P}=\mathbb{E}_{C^{P}}[\mathcal{A}^{C^{P}}]
and
\bar{\mathcal{B}}=\underset{z\sim P}{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{B}(z)]
.

Proof.

For
V
such that
V|0\rangle=|P\rangle
,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{P}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{C^{P}}[\mathcal{A}^{C^{P}}]=\underset{W,C^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{WVC^{\prime}}\right]=\underset{W_{1},W_{2},C^{\prime},f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{W_{1}ff^{-1}W_{2}VC^{\prime}}\right]=\underset{W_{1},W_{2},C^{\prime},f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{W_{1}f(f^{-1}W_{2}f)f^{-1}VC^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{W_{1},W_{2},C^{\prime},f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{W_{1}fW_{2}(f^{-1}V)C^{\prime}}\right]=\underset{W_{1},W_{2},C^{\prime},f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathcal{A}^{W_{1}f(W_{2}U_{f}C^{\prime})}\right],

(III.57)

where the first equality is the definition, the second equality uses the definition of how
C^{P}
is sampled as described above Theorem
18
where
W
is the random phase and
C^{\prime}
is a random unitary for which
|0\rangle
is stationary, the third equality averages over a uniformly random function
f
that permutes all bitstrings and holds due to the fact that applying one random phase is equivalent to applying two random phases, the fifth equality uses the fact that the measure of random phases does not change when conjugated by a random permutation
f
, and the last equality uses newly defined unitaries
U_{f}=f^{-1}V
.

Since
W_{2}U_{f}C^{\prime}
is a trivial quantum algorithm calling
W_{2}U_{f}C^{\prime}
, by Theorem
18
, there exists a quantum channel
\Xi
given
(k+\ell)
samples drawn from the bitstring probability distributions
P_{U_{f}}
of
U_{f}|0\rangle
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\underset{W_{2},C^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}[W_{2}U_{f}C^{\prime}]-\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k}\right)^{k}\right)}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1},

(III.58)

where
\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}=\underset{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\sim p_{U}}{\mathbb{E}}[\Xi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})]
.
To clarify what kind of mathematical object
\Xi
is, since
W_{2}U_{f}C^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
is a quantum channel on
n
-qubits, where
\mathcal{H}
is the
n
-qubit Hilbert space and
\mathcal{L}
represents the set of bounded linear operators, we know
\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
and
\Xi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k}):\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. More explicitly,
\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}
internally sample
z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\sim p_{U_{f}}
, which controls the action of
\Xi
.

Having access to samples from
P_{U_{f}}
is equivalent to having access to samples from
P_{V}
followed by permutation
f^{-1}
. Therefore,
\bar{\Xi}
can be modeled by a quantum channel

\displaystyle\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}=\underset{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\sim P_{V}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Xi(f^{-1}(z_{1}),\dots,f^{-1}(z_{k}))\right].

(III.59)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\underset{W_{1},f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[W_{1}\circ f\circ\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}\right]=\underset{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\sim P_{V}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{W_{1},f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[W_{1}\circ f\circ\Xi(f^{-1}(z_{1}),\dots,f^{-1}(z_{k}))\right]\right],

(III.60)

where
f
now also means the unitary or quantum channel on
n
-qubit quantum states that perform the remapping
f
of the bitstrings basis states. The above expression is a quantum channel for
n
-qubit states. Denote
\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}
conditioned on the
k
samples not repeating (which we call event
R^{c}
where
R
is the event of repeating and the superscript
c
indicates the complement of the set) as
\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}_{R^{c}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\underset{W_{1},f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[W_{1}\circ f\circ\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}\right]-\underset{W_{1},f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[W_{1}\circ f\circ\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}_{R^{c}}\right]\right\|_{\diamond}\leq\Pr[R].

(III.61)

We now analyze the effect of the channel
\underset{f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\circ\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}_{R^{c}}\right]
. To do this, we focus on the special case where the input quantum state to the channel is an
n
-qubit state not entangled with any other quantum systems. In this case, the output is an
n
-qubit state which depends on the input state. We denote the output of
\underset{f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\circ\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}\right]
as
\rho
and that of
\underset{f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\circ\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}_{R^{c}}\right]
as
\rho_{R^{c}}
. We have

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=\underset{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}[\rho_{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}}]

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=\Pr[R^{c}]\cdot\rho_{R^{c}}+\Pr[R]\cdot\rho_{R}

(III.62)

\displaystyle\rho_{R^{c}}

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})\in B}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\rho_{(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\right]

(III.63)

\displaystyle\rho_{R}

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}\in C}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\rho_{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}}\right]

(III.64)

\displaystyle\rho_{(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\in S((z_{1},\dots,z_{k}))}\rho_{s_{1},\dots,s_{k}}

(III.65)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\sum_{f}\Pr[f]\cdot f(\Xi(\{f^{-1}(\pi(z_{i}))\}_{i\in[k]})),

(III.66)

where
B
is the set of all unordered sets of
k
unique bitstrings,
C
is the set of all ordered lists of
k
bitstrings with repeated entries,
S((z_{1},\dots,z_{k}))
is the set of all possible reorderings of the
k
non-repeating bitstrings in
(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
, and
\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
is the set of all functions that map the set of bitstrings in
(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
onto itself and act trivially on all other bitstrings. We use
(\cdots)
to denote an unordered set and
\{\cdots\}
to denote an ordered list.

For each
\pi
and
f
, we can define
f^{\prime}
such that
f^{-1}\circ\pi=f^{\prime-1}
, which implies
f=\pi\circ f^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\rho_{(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\sum_{f^{\prime}}\Pr[f^{\prime}]\cdot\pi(f^{\prime}(\Xi(\{f^{\prime-1}(z_{i})\}_{i\in[k]})))

(III.67)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{f}\Pr[f]\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\pi(f(\Xi(\{f^{-1}(z_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}))).

(III.68)

For a generic state

\displaystyle\Xi(\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}|\alpha\rangle\langle\beta|\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(\alpha,\beta),

(III.69)

we can write

\displaystyle\langle x|\rho_{(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}|y\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\underset{(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{f:\{f^{-1}(z_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(\alpha,\beta)\langle x|\pi f(\alpha)\rangle\langle\pi f(\beta)|y\rangle\right]\right],

(III.70)

where
(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})
are uniformly sampled sets of non-repeating
k
bitstrings. We can write the sum this way because enumeration over all
f
means that all possible sets of non-repeating samples
(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})
appear. Further,

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(\alpha,\beta)\langle x|\pi f(\alpha)\rangle\langle\pi f(\beta)|y\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(x),f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(y)).

(III.71)

In the case where
x,y\in\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
and
x=y
, we have
\pi^{-1}(x)\in\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
and
f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(x)\in\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
. Summing over all
\pi
means each
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
appears the same number of times. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(x),f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(y))=\frac{k!}{k}\sum_{i\in[k]}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s_{i},s_{i}).

(III.72)

For
x\neq y
, summing over all
\pi
means every possible pair by choosing from
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
appears the same number of times, except those equal pairs. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(x),f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(y))=\frac{k!}{k(k-1)}\sum_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s_{i},s_{j}).

(III.73)

In the case where
x,y\notin\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
and
x=y
, we have
\pi^{-1}(x)=x
and
f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(x)\notin\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
. Averaging over all
f:\{f^{-1}(z_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
, all samples not in
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
appears with equal probability. Therefore,

\displaystyle\underset{f:\{f^{-1}(z_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(x),f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(y))\right]=\frac{k!}{N-k}\sum_{s\notin\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s,s).

(III.74)

For
x\neq y
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{f:\{f^{-1}(z_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(x),f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(y))\right]=\frac{k!}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}\notin\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]},s_{1}\neq s_{2}}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s_{1},s_{2}).

(III.75)

For
x\in\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
and
y\notin\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
,

\displaystyle\underset{f:\{f^{-1}(z_{i})\}_{i\in[k]}=\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(x),f^{-1}\pi^{-1}(y))\right]=\frac{k!}{k(N-k)}\sum_{s_{1}\in\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]},s_{2}\notin\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s_{1},s_{2}),

(III.76)

and for
x\in\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
and
y\notin\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
, the value is the complex conjugate.

Collecting all cases,

\displaystyle\langle x|\rho_{(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}|y\rangle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{k}\underset{(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i\in[k]}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s_{i},s_{i})\right]&\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ x=y\wedge x\in\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\\
\frac{1}{k(k-1)}\underset{(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i,j\in[k],i\neq j}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s_{i},s_{j})\right]&\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ x\neq y\wedge x,y\in\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\\
\frac{1}{N-k}\underset{(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{s\notin\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s,s)\right]&\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ x=y\wedge x,y\notin\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\\
\frac{1}{(N-k)(N-k-1)}\underset{(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}\notin\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]},s_{1}\neq s_{2}}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s_{1},s_{2})\right]&\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ x\neq y\wedge x,y\notin\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\\
\frac{1}{k(N-k)}\sum_{s_{1}\in\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]},s_{2}\notin\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s_{1},s_{2})&\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ x\in\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]},y\notin\{z_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\\
\frac{1}{k(N-k)}\sum_{s_{1}\notin\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]},s_{2}\in\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s_{1},s_{2})&\penalty\ \text{otherwise.}\\
\end{cases}

(III.77)

The special case of the classical state discussed before corresponds to the special case where the non-diagonal terms are zero. Additionally, the values of the matrix elements are independent of the samples
z_{1},\dots,z_{k}
although the positions of these terms obviously depend on the samples.

Then, averaging over all
(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
, we have

\displaystyle\rho_{R^{c}}=\underset{(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\rho_{(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}\right],

(III.78)

whose diagonal elements are

\displaystyle\langle x|\rho_{R^{c}}|x\rangle=\frac{u}{k}\cdot p^{R^{c}}_{V}(x)+\frac{1-u}{N-k}\cdot\left(1-p^{R^{c}}_{V}(x)\right),

(III.79)

where we used the definitions

\displaystyle p^{R_{c}}_{V}(x)

\displaystyle=\Pr_{(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})}[x\in(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})]

(III.80)

\displaystyle u=\underset{(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i\in[k]}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s_{i},s_{i})\right]

\displaystyle=1-\underset{(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{s\notin\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}\xi_{\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}}(s,s)\right].

(III.81)

Finally, averaging over all phases, the state
\underset{W_{1}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[W_{1}\rho_{R^{c}}W_{1}^{\dagger}\right]
becomes classical and all non-diagonal elements vanish. Therefore, to prepare the state, we can simply perform the following sampling process: first, sample a random set of non-repeating
k
bitstrings
(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
with probability proportional to
\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{V}(z_{i})
; then with probability
u
, sample a random bitstring from the set. Otherwise, sample a random bitstring not in the set. The first case is equivalent to drawing a random
z
from the set conditioned on
R^{c}
, whose probability we denote as
\Pr[z|R^{c}]
. We denote the sampling processing corresponding to
\underset{W_{1}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[W_{1}\rho_{R^{c}}W_{1}^{\dagger}\right]
as
\mathrm{Sim}_{R^{c}}
.

We now show that the probability distribution of
z
for
\underset{W_{1}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[W_{1}\rho_{R^{c}}W_{1}^{\dagger}\right]
is close to a mixture of one random sample from
p_{V}
with probability
u
and one random sample from the uniform distribution with probability
1-u
. Consider another sampling process
A
: sample
z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\sim p_{V}
. With probability
u
, choose a random
i\in[k]
and output
z_{i}
. Otherwise, output a random bitstring not in
\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}
. The probability of outputting
z
is

\displaystyle p_{A}(z)=\Pr[R^{c}]\cdot\Pr[z|R^{c}]+\Pr[R]\cdot\Pr[z|R],

(III.82)

so the total variation distance (TVD) between
A
and
\mathrm{Sim}_{R^{c}}
is at most
\Pr[R]
. Now consider another sampling process
B
: with probability
u
, output a random sample from
p_{V}
; otherwise, output a random bitstring from the uniform distribution. An equivalent process of
B
is as follows: sample
z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\sim p_{V}
. With probability
u
, choose a random
i\in[k]
and output
z_{i}
. Otherwise, output a random bitstring from the uniform distribution. Only the second case differs from the sampling process
A
. Denote the second case as
S
, the probability distributions for this case is

\displaystyle p_{A}(z|S)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N-k}\Pr_{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}}[\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}\not\ni z]

(III.83)

\displaystyle p_{B}(z|S)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}=\frac{1}{N}\Pr_{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}}[\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}\not\ni z]+\frac{1}{N}\Pr_{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}}[\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}\ni z]

(III.84)

\displaystyle\big|p_{B}(z|S)-p_{A}(z|S)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\left(\frac{1}{1-k/N}-1\right)\Pr_{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}}[\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}\not\ni z]+\frac{1}{N}\Pr_{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}}[\{z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\}\ni z]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\left(\frac{1}{1-k/N}-1\right)+\frac{1}{N}\cdot k\cdot p_{V}(z).

(III.85)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TVD}(A,B)

\displaystyle=(1-u)\sum_{z}\left[\big|p_{B}(z|S)-p_{A}(z|S)\big|\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1-u)\sum_{z}\left[\frac{1}{N}\left(\frac{1}{1-k/N}-1\right)+\frac{1}{N}\cdot k\cdot p_{V}(z)\right]

\displaystyle=(1-u)\left[\left(\frac{1}{1-k/N}-1\right)+k/N\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2k}{N(1-k/N)}.

(III.86)

Therefore, the total TVD between
B
and
\mathrm{Sim}_{R^{c}}
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{TVD}(\mathrm{Sim}_{R^{c}},B)\leq\Pr[R]+\frac{2k}{N(1-k/N)}.

(III.87)

Although we only analyzed the special case where the input to the channel is an
n
-qubit state unentangled with any other quantum systems, the result shows that the action of the channel is close to sampling
z
from
B
, where the probability
u
depends on the input quantum state. The result can be immediately extended to the case where the input to the channel is entangled to a quantum register held by an adversary. Notice that while the channel
\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}
and the channel
f
acts coherently, the channel
W_{1}
will apply a random phase on the state, thus breaking any entanglement between the oracle’s output and the adversary’s quantum register. In other words, the state shared between the oracle’s output and the adversary’s side information can only be correlated classically, which implies that it is optimal to employ shared randomness to prepare this state. Therefore, our earlier claim also covers the case where the adversary prepares quantum states that are initially entangled. Denoting the quantum channel that corresponds to the sampling process
B
which has access to one random sample from
p_{V}
as
\bar{\mathcal{B}}=\underset{z\sim p_{V}}{\mathbb{E}}[\mathcal{B}(z)]
,

\displaystyle\left\|\underset{W_{1},f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[W_{1}\circ f\circ\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}_{R^{c}}\right]-\bar{\mathcal{B}}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq\Pr[R]+\frac{2k}{N(1-k/N)}.

(III.88)

Using Eq.
III.61
,

\displaystyle\left\|\underset{W_{1},f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[W_{1}\circ f\circ\bar{\Xi}^{P_{U_{f}}}\right]-\bar{\mathcal{B}}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq 2\Pr[R]+\frac{2k}{N(1-k/N)}.

(III.89)

Finally, using Eq.
III.58
,

\displaystyle\left\|\underset{W_{1},W_{2},C^{\prime},f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[W_{1}fW_{2}U_{f}C^{\prime}\right]-\bar{\mathcal{B}}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2(1-(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k})^{k})}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}+\frac{2(k+\ell)(k+\ell-1)}{N}+\frac{2k}{N(1-k/N)},

(III.90)

where we used the fact that

\displaystyle\Pr[R]=1-\Pr_{m\sim\Phi(N,k+\ell)}[\|m\|_{\infty}=1]\leq\frac{(k+\ell)(k+\ell-1)}{N}.

(III.91)

Specifically,
\Pr_{m\sim\Phi(N,k+\ell)}[\|m\|_{\infty}=1]
is the probability that a random frequency vector sampled uniformly from all possible frequency vectors has infinite norm
1
as defined in the proof of Theorem
19
and substituting
k\rightarrow k+\ell
, which is the same as the probability of obtaining non-repeating samples averaged over samples and distributions
P
from the Dirichlet distribution.

∎

As a corollary, we can obtain an improved entropy bound for the general adversary for the special case of
m=1
and
T=1
.

Corollary 27
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be any algorithm making
(1,1)
-queries to
U\sim\mathrm{Haar}(2^{n})
. If
n\geq 50
,
k\leq N^{-1/2}
,
n\leq 10^{6}
, and
\mathcal{A}
solves
(1+\delta)-\mathrm{XHOG}_{m}
, then

H(Z|UE)_{\mathcal{A}^{U}}\geq\left(\delta-2m\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)-1.001\times\frac{16^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}\right)n-\log_{2}n-5

(III.92)

and
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}=\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k}\right)^{k}\right)}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}+\frac{2(k+\ell)(k+\ell-1)}{N}+\frac{2k}{N(1-k/N)}
.

Proof.

We follow similar arguments in
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
. By Theorem
26
, for every
(1,1)
-query
\mathcal{A}
, there exists
\mathcal{B}
given access to one sample the circuit (drawn according the probability distribution induced by the circuit) such that
\|\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{P}-\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{P}\|_{\diamond}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
. Denote the output state of
\bar{\mathcal{B}}
as
\Psi
. Also, by similar arguments as Theorem
23
, we can show that
\mathcal{B}
solves
b-\mathrm{XHOG}_{m}
for
b=1+\delta-2\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}(n\ln 2+3)
. Combining the aforementioned result, Corollary
21
and Theorem
11
, this yields

H(Z|UE)_{\Psi}\geq\left(\delta-2m\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}(n\ln 2+3)-1.001\times\frac{16\ln^{2}N}{N}\right)n-\log_{2}n-3.

(III.93)

This provides a bound on a state
\Psi
close to the actual state of the algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{U}
. We can now use the continuity bound of entropy in Lemma
24
to bound the entropy of the actual state. The dimension of the first system is
2^{mn}
, so entropy is reduced by
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}n+(1+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})h\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}}{1+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}}\right)\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}n+2
, which completes the proof.
∎

III.5
Summary of improvements

Overall we make improvements in the correction terms of the single-round entropy. The improvements valid for general
T
query adversaries are reducing
(k+\ell)^{3}\rightarrow(k+\ell)^{2}
and
2\log_{2}(k+\ell)\rightarrow\log_{2}(k+\ell)
. The improvements valid only for one-access adversaries are reducing
(k+\ell)^{3}\rightarrow(k+\ell)^{2}
and
2\log_{2}(k+\ell)\rightarrow 0
. For the main text figure, the baseline we compare our one-access adversaries to is the theory of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
with explicit constants as shown in Theorem
7
.

III.6
Effect of classical simulation

The above analysis shows the entropy guarantee of a quantum device with query access to the challenge circuits. However, since we are working with finite-size challenge circuits, the adversary may be able to simulate the circuits with a non-negligible fidelity, and we need to consider the classical simulation capabilities. Consider the case where the adversary simulates
m^{\prime}
out of
m
circuits classically, and each simulated circuit is expected to solve
(1+\delta_{j})-\mathrm{XHOG}
over the Haar measure, meaning the
m^{\prime}
simulated circuits are expected to solve
(1+\delta^{\prime})-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m^{\prime}}
, where
\delta^{\prime}=\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\sum_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}}\delta_{j}
. Note that this situation allows simulations for different circuits to be dependent on each other, although there are no known ways for this to be meaningfully helpful.

If all
m
circuits, quantum and classical, solve
(1+\delta)-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
for
\delta>\delta^{\prime}
, then the remaining
m^{\prime\prime}=m-m^{\prime}
quantum samples must solve
(1+\delta^{\prime\prime})-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m^{\prime\prime}}
with

\delta^{\prime\prime}=\frac{1}{1-m^{\prime}/m}\left(\delta-\frac{m^{\prime}}{m}\delta^{\prime}\right)=\frac{\delta-F_{\rm C}}{1-m^{\prime}/m},

(III.94)

and
F_{\rm C}=\delta^{\prime}m^{\prime}/m
can be interpreted as the average classical simulation fidelity per circuit. The entropy of the
m^{\prime\prime}=m-m^{\prime}=m(1-m^{\prime}/m)
quantum samples, and therefore the entropy of all
m
samples since classical samples contribute no entropy, is

\displaystyle H(\vec{Z}|\vec{C}E)

\displaystyle\geq m^{\prime\prime}\left[\left(\delta^{\prime\prime}-2m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)-1.001\frac{16(k+\ell)^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}\right)n-\log_{2}n-\log_{2}(k+\ell)-3\right]-2

\displaystyle=m(1-m^{\prime}/m)\left[\left(\frac{\delta-F_{\rm C}}{1-m^{\prime}/m}-2m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)-1.001\frac{16(k+\ell)^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}\right)n-\log_{2}n-\log_{2}(k+\ell)-3\right]-2

\displaystyle\geq m\left[\left(\delta-F_{C}-2m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)-1.001\frac{16(k+\ell)^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}\right)n-\log_{2}n-\log_{2}(k+\ell)-3\right]-2.

(III.95)

It is then sufficient for the client to upper bound
F_{\rm C}
. For example, for frugal rejection sampling based on contracting a fraction of slices of the tensor network corresponding to the probability amplitude, which is the finite-size adversary analyzed in
Liu et al. 2025
, the average simulation fidelity is
F_{\rm C}=\frac{C_{\rm eff}T_{\rm batch}\mathcal{A}}{m\mathcal{B}}
, where
C_{\rm eff}
is the numerical efficiency of the adversary,
T_{\rm batch}
is the allowed time for the adversary to return all samples since receiving the batch of circuits,
\mathcal{A}
is the adversary’s classical computational power, and
\mathcal{B}
is the cost of exactly simulating one circuit. For an adversary that computes
2^{k}
amplitudes by contraction a fraction
f
of slices, which we discuss in more detail in a later section, the fidelity is
F_{\rm C}=f(k-1)
. Similarly, given the total time per batch and the assumed adversarial computational power, one can in principle upper bound
F_{\rm C}
for any classical simulation algorithm.

IV
Multi-round security under non-adaptive hybrid adversaries

In this section, we analyze an adversary whose output across rounds are independent, though not necessarily identical. For convenience, we only consider classical side information for both the multi-round output and single-round outputs. This is justified because, in settings where the randomness is used publicly, certified randomness is concerned only with entropy conditioned on an initial snapshot of classical information. This is the case for multi-party protocols, but also for randomness amplification where the quantum randomness is not assumed to be private. Conversely, in settings where the certified randomness needs to remain private, the client must have physical control over the quantum device, and any shared initial entanglement can be destroyed by simple measures such as powering down the quantum device.

To motivate the non-adaptive adversary, we observe that past output bitstrings do not aid future adversarial interactions since future challenge circuits are generated independently. The exception to this would be if the adversary computes the potential contribution to the XEB score, which may allow the adversary to use lower entropy samples if it knows the past samples are likely to do well on XEB. In the limit where the number of rounds is large, the fraction of verified circuits is small, and the classical simulation fidelity the adversary can achieve in the limited time is small, computing the effect on the XEB score has little effect. If the history is short, the probability that these samples will be selected for validation is small, which means the adversary should not change the strategy very much depending on the history. If the history is long, then with high probability the history should look ‘typical’. If the classical simulation fidelity is small, then the contribution to the XEB score cannot be accurately characterized.

IV.1
Preliminaries

We first define some notations before proceeding with the formal analysis. A quantum state
\rho
is a positive-semidefinite operator with
\Tr(\rho)=1
. A multipartite state
\rho
with a classical subsystem over register
X
and a quantum subsystem over register
A
is called a classical-quantum state, and has the form

\rho_{XA}=\sum_{x}p(x)\outerproduct{x}{x}\otimes\rho_{A}^{x},

(IV.1)

where
\sum_{x}p(x)=1
. One may also be interested in analyzing subnormalized classical-quantum states, which may arise when we condition a normalized state on some event
\Omega
on the classical register
X
:

\rho_{XA\wedge\Omega}=\sum_{x\in\Omega}p(x)\outerproduct{x}{x}\otimes\rho_{A}^{x},

(IV.2)

where the probability of event
\Omega
is
\Pr[\Omega]=\sum_{x\in\Omega}p(x)
. Once normalized, the above state is given by

\rho_{XA|\Omega}=\Pr[\Omega]^{-1}\rho_{XA\wedge\Omega}.

(IV.3)

For cryptographic analysis, it is typical to consider the worst-case probability of an adversary guessing the outcome of a source of randomness. For a classical system, this corresponds to the probability of the mostly likely outcome, the negative logarithm of which is called the min-entropy.

However, this definition is not applicable to quantum systems and requires generalization. For certified randomness, since the random outputs are always classical but the adversary side information might be quantum, we are interested in the min-entropy for a classical-quantum state which is discussed in Section 6.14 of Ref.
Tomamichel 2015
.

Definition 28
.

Given a (potentially subnormalized) classical-quantum state
\rho_{XA}=\sum_{x}p(x)\outerproduct{x}{x}\otimes\rho_{A}^{x}
, the conditional min-entropy
H_{\min}(X|A)_{\rho}
is given by

H_{\min}(X|A)_{\rho}=-\log_{2}p_{\rm guess}(X|A)_{\rho},

(IV.4)

where
p_{\rm guess}(X|A)_{\rho}:=\sup_{\{M_{x}\}_{x}}\sum_{x}p(x)\lVert\rho_{A}^{x}M_{x}\rVert_{1},
with supremum over POVMs on register
A
, is known as the guessing probability.

The min-entropy can be generalized by including a smoothing parameter.

Definition 29
.

Given a classical-quantum state
\rho_{XA}
and a smoothing parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,\sqrt{\lVert\rho_{XA}\rVert_{1}})
, the conditional
\varepsilon
-min-entropy is given by

H_{\min}^{\varepsilon}(X|A)_{\rho}:=\sup_{\sigma}H_{\min}(X|A)_{\sigma}\,,

(IV.5)

where the supremum is over states
\sigma_{XA}
in the
\varepsilon
-ball in purified distance centered around
\rho

Tomamichel 2015
.

IV.2
Single-round statistical properties

For the classical part of the adversary, we consider a specific class of adversaries whose probability distribution of the output bitstring probability amplitudes can be explicitly modeled. This allows us to compute the probability distribution of the XEB score of a protocol in Fig.
6
.

We first obtain the following result.

Protocol Arguments:

\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Number of qubits}

\displaystyle m\in\mathbb{N}\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Number of circuits per round (batch size)}

\displaystyle L\in\mathbb{N}\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Number of rounds}

\displaystyle T_{\rm batch}\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Threshold on the time allowed for the samples to return}

\displaystyle L_{\rm val}\in[L]\penalty\

Number of validation circuits

\displaystyle p_{\rm max}\in(1/2^{n},1]\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Maximum contribution probability}

\displaystyle s^{*}\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Threshold for test score}

The Protocol:

1.

Set
s=0
.

2.

For
i\in[L]
, do:

(a)

Sample
m
Haar random unitaries.

(b)

Send them to the server and start the timer.

(c)

If does not receive the bitstrings in time
T_{\rm batch}
, abort. Otherwise, store the bitstrings
\vec{Z}_{i}
.

3.

Sample validation sets of batch indices
i
uniformly from all possible size
L_{\rm val}
subsets of
[L]
.

4.

For each
i\in\mathcal{V}
, uniformly sample one of the
m
circuit and bitstring. Compute the bitstring probability
p
for the chosen validation circuit of that batch, and add
\frac{N\min(p,p_{\rm max})}{L_{\rm val}}
to
s
. Finally, abort if
s<s^{*}
.

Figure 6:
Description of the protocol for the ad hoc analysis.

Lemma 30
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be any algorithm making
(T,m)
-queries to
\vec{U}\sim\mathrm{Haar}^{\otimes m}
. If
n\geq 50,k\leq N^{-1/2},m,n\leq 10^{6}
, and

H(\vec{Z}|\vec{U}E)_{\Psi}\leq h,

(IV.6)

then there exists a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{F}
which, given
(k+\ell)
samples drawn from
P^{(i)}
for all
i\in[m]
, outputs a state
m\varepsilon^{\prime}
-close to the output of
\mathcal{A}
and cannot solve
(1+\delta)-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
, where

\delta=\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{h+2}{m}+\log_{2}n+\log_{2}(k+\ell)+3\right)+4m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)+1.001\times\frac{16(k+\ell)^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N},

(IV.7)

and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is as defined in Theorem
25
.

Proof.

Theorem
25
implies that if entropy is at most
h
, then
\mathcal{A}
cannot solve
(1+\delta^{\prime})-\mathrm{MXHOG}
with

\delta^{\prime}=\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{h+2}{m}+\log_{2}n+\log_{2}(k+\ell)+3\right)+2m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)+1.001\times\frac{16(k+\ell)^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}.

(IV.8)

Combining this with Theorem
23
completes the proof.
∎

We also have a lemma similar to Theorem
19
.

Lemma 31
.

For a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{F}
given
k
samples drawn from
P^{(i)}
for all
i\in[m]
, if

\underset{\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{z}\sim\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\vec{P}}(\rho)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]\leq m\frac{2-\varepsilon}{N+k},

(IV.9)

then

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}\Pr\left[z^{(i)}\in\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k]}\right]\leq 1-\varepsilon.

(IV.10)

Proof.

Similar to Theorem
19
,

\underset{\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N}+f^{(i)})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]=\underset{\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{f_{z^{(i)}}^{(i)}+1}{N+k}\right]\geq\frac{1}{N+k}\left(1+\underset{\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\Pr}\left[f_{z^{(i)}}^{(i)}>0\right]\right).

(IV.11)

The score of
\mathcal{F}
in expectation is

\displaystyle\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N}+\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]

(IV.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[m]}\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N}+\vec{f})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]

(IV.13)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[m]}\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\frac{1}{N+k}\left(1+\underset{\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\Pr}\left[f_{z^{(i)}}^{(i)}>0\right]\right)

(IV.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N+k}\left(m+\sum_{i\in[m]}\left(\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m},\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\Pr}\left[f^{(i)}_{z^{(i)}}>0\right]\right)\right).

(IV.15)

Thus if the score is at most
m\frac{2-\varepsilon}{N+k}
,

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}\underset{\vec{f}\sim\Phi(N,k)^{\otimes m}\vec{z}\sim Q(\vec{f})}{\Pr}\left[f_{z^{(i)}}^{(i)}>0\right]\leq 1-\varepsilon.

(IV.16)

∎

Lemma
30
and
31
implies the following lemma.

Lemma 32
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be any algorithm making
(T,m)
-queries to
\vec{U}\sim\mathrm{Haar}^{\otimes m}
. If
n\geq 50,k\leq N^{-1/2},m,n\leq 10^{6}
, and

H(\vec{Z}|\vec{U}E)_{\Psi}\leq h,

(IV.17)

then there exists a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{F}
given
(k+\ell)
samples drawn from
P^{(i)}
for all
i\in[m]
such that the output state is
m\varepsilon^{\prime}
-close to the output of
\mathcal{A}
and

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}\Pr\left[z^{(i)}\in\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k+\ell]}\right]\leq\delta,

(IV.18)

where

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=\frac{h+2}{mn}+C,

(IV.19)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left(\log_{2}n+\log_{2}(k+\ell)+3\right)+4m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)+1.001\times\frac{16(k+\ell)^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}+0.001.

(IV.20)

Proof.

Lemma
30
implies that
\mathcal{F}
cannot solve
(1+\delta)-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
, then

\underset{\vec{P}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N})^{\otimes m}}{\mathbb{E}}\underset{\vec{z}\sim\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\vec{P}}(\rho)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i\in[m]}P^{(i)}(z^{(i)})\right]\leq m\frac{2-\varepsilon}{N+k}=m\frac{1+\delta}{N}\rightarrow\varepsilon=2-\frac{N+k}{N}(1+\delta).

(IV.21)

By Lemma
31
,

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in[m]}\Pr\left[z^{(i)}\in\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k+\ell]}\right]\leq 1-\varepsilon=\delta+\frac{k}{N}(1+\delta)\leq\delta+10^{-22}.

(IV.22)

The negligible constant is due to the fact that
