n\geq 50
and
k\leq N^{-1/2}
, and can be safely absorbed into the constant
0.001
in
\delta
.
∎

Let us consider the simple case where the probability amplitudes for all rounds are independent. Consider the case where the bitstring probability distributions for all rounds are fixed except the
i
th round. Recall that to the adversary, higher XHOG score is better. If the
i
th round has two possible bitstring probability distributions, it is always more favorable for the adversary to choose the
first-order stochastically dominant
probability distribution.

Definition 33
.

A random variable
A
on
\mathbb{R}
has first-order stochastic dominance over a random variable
B
on
\mathbb{R}
if
F_{B}(x)\geq F_{A}(x)
for all
x
and
F_{B}(x)>F_{A}(x)
for some
x
, where
F_{A}(x)=\Pr[A\leq x]
and
F_{B}(x)=\Pr[B\leq x]
are the cumulative density functions of
A
and
B
. For probability distributions
\rho_{A}
of
A
and
\rho_{B}
of
B
, the dominance of random variable
A
over random variable
B
is denoted as
\rho_{A}\succeq\rho_{B}
.

The following lemma will be useful to analyze the cumulative density functions of the bitstring probabilities of various processes we will discuss below.

Lemma 34
.

The cumulative density function of the following process:

1.

With probability
1-q
, sample
Q\sim\Gamma(1,1)
.

2.

Otherwise, sample
Q\sim\Gamma(2,1)
,

has the following cumulative density function:

F(x)=1-(1+qx)e^{-x}.

(IV.23)

Now we analyze the single-round output of a sample access algorithm
\mathcal{F}
.

Lemma 35
.

Consider a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{F}
given
k
samples drawn from
P^{(i)}
for all
i\in[m]
. Randomly select one of the
m
outputs of the algorithm and compute the bitstring probability
p
and cap the value at
p_{\rm max}
. Then, the probability distribution
\rho_{p}^{\prime}
with the following cumulative density function dominates
\rho_{p}
:

F(p)=\begin{cases}0&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }0\leq p\leq p_{\rm min}\\
(1-(1+qN(1-N^{-1/3})p)e^{-N(1-N^{-1/3})p})-(mN^{-1/3}+mk^{2}/N)&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }p_{\rm min}<p<p_{\rm max}\\
1&\mathrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

(IV.24)

where

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-k^{2}/N}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{b\in[m]}\Pr[z^{(b)}\in\{z^{(b)}_{c}\}_{c\in[k]}]

(IV.25)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(1-(1+qNp_{\rm min})e^{-Np_{\rm min}})-(mN^{-1/3}+mk^{2}/N).

(IV.26)

Proof.

We use the same notation as Theorem
19
for the frequency vector
f^{(i)}
of the samples. Conditioned on the observed frequency vector, the posterior distribution is
P^{(i)}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{N}+f^{(i)})
. Sampling
P^{(i)}
can be done using the procedure discussed in Section 3.4.1 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, which we paraphrase here. We first sample
Q_{j}\sim\Gamma(1+f_{j}^{(i)},1)
independently for
j\in[N]
, where
\Gamma(\alpha,\beta)
is the Gamma distribution with parameters
\alpha,\beta
. Then, we set
P^{(i)}(\mathrm{str}(j))=Q_{j}/\bar{Q}
, where
\mathrm{str}(j)
is the
j
th bitstring in
\{0,1\}^{N}
and
\bar{Q}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}Q_{j}
. As shown in Eq. 199 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
,
\Pr[\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=1]\geq 1-\frac{k^{2}}{N}
over choices of frequency vectors.

Let us focus on attention on the special where there is only one distribution
P^{(i)}
. Denote the
k
samples the from
P^{(i)}
given to algorithm
\mathcal{F}
as
\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k]}
. There are two possibilities for the
k
samples. On possibility is that the samples do not repeat, which means
\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=1
since
f^{(i)}
is the frequency vector of the samples. The other possibility is
\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}>1
, which we ignore for the time being since it is very unlikely. For the output
z^{(i)}
of algorithm
\mathcal{F}
, there are also two possibilities, namely it is or is not from the set of input samples
\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k]}
.

The
z^{(i)}\in\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k]}
and
\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=1
case.

The bitstring probability
p
can be sampled by sampling
Q\sim\Gamma(2,1)
, sample
Q_{j}\sim\Gamma(1,1)
for
j\in[N-k]
, and sample
Q_{\ell}\sim\Gamma(2,1)
for
\ell\in[k-1]
, and return

p=Q\cdot\left(Q+\sum_{j\in[N-k]}Q_{j}+\sum_{\ell\in[k-1]}Q_{\ell}\right)^{-1}\leq Q\cdot\left(\sum_{j\in[N-k]}Q_{j}+\sum_{\ell\in[k-1]}Q_{\ell}\right)^{-1}.

(IV.27)

We denote the corresponding probability density function (PDF) of this distribution as
f(p;N,k)
. The sum in parenthesis concentrates to
N+k
, which means

f(p;N,k)\approx N^{2}xe^{-Np}.

(IV.28)

This is equivalent to saying that
f
is very close to the PDF of the bitstring probability of sampling honestly from a perfect quantum state
T. A. N. Brandão and Peralta 2020
;
Morvan et al. 2024
;
Liu et al. 2025
. Moreover, with integer shape parameters
\alpha
, Gamma distributions are Erlang distributions. By the property of sum of independent Erlang distributed variables (PDF of the sum is Erlang distributed with the same rate
\beta
and the sum of the shape parameters as the shape parameter),

\sum_{j\in[N-k]}Q_{j}+\sum_{\ell\in[k-1]}Q_{\ell}\sim\Gamma(N+k-2,1).

(IV.29)

Further,
\Gamma(N+k-2,1)
dominates
\Gamma(N,1)
. Therefore, if we sample
p^{\prime}
by sampling
Q\sim\Gamma(2,1)
and
Q^{\prime}\sim\Gamma(N,1)
and setting
p^{\prime}=Q/Q^{\prime}
, then
p^{\prime}
should dominate
p
. We therefore only consider the sampling process for
p^{\prime}
.

Since the mean and variance of
\Gamma(N,1)
are both
N
, the Chebyshev’s inequality implies

\Pr[\left|Q^{\prime}-N\geq c\sqrt{N}\right|]\leq\frac{1}{c^{2}}.

(IV.30)

Setting
c=N^{1/6}
yields

\Pr[Q^{\prime}/N\leq 1-N^{-1/3}]\leq N^{-1/3}.

(IV.31)

Therefore, in the case of
Q^{\prime}/N>1-N^{-1/3}
, we have
p^{\prime}<\frac{Q}{N(1-N^{-1/3})}
.

The following sampling process, which assumes
Q^{\prime}/N>1-N^{-1/3}
, is at most
N^{-1/3}
away from the actual sampling process of
p^{\prime}
which dominates
p
: sample
Q\sim\Gamma(2,1)
and set
p^{\prime}=\frac{Q}{N(1-N^{-1/3})}
.

The
z^{(i)}\notin\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k]}
and
\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=1
case.

We can sample
p
by sampling
Q
and
Q_{j}
from
\Gamma(1,1)
for
j\in[N-1]
and set

p=Q\cdot\left(Q+\sum_{j\in[N-1]}Q_{j}\right)^{-1}.

(IV.32)

The term in the parenthesis should concentrate to
N
, which means

f(p;N,k)\approx Ne^{-Np}.

(IV.33)

This is equivalent to saying that
f
is very close to the PDF of the bitstring probability of sampling honestly from the uniform distribution
T. A. N. Brandão and Peralta 2020
;
Morvan et al. 2024
;
Liu et al. 2025
. By similar arguments as in the previous case, the following sampling process, which assumes
Q^{\prime}/N>1-N^{-1/3}
, is at most
N^{-1/3}
away from the sampling process of another random variable
p^{\prime}
which dominates
p
: set
p^{\prime}=\frac{Q}{N(1-N^{-1/3})}
, where
Q\sim\Gamma(1,1)
.

The general case for
m
samples when
\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=1
for all
i\in[m]
.

Above, we only examined the sampling of one bitstring probability from a single round of
\mathcal{A}
with
m
circuits. In general, however, the outputs for different circuits can be correlated.

For a binary bitstring
\vec{s}\in\{0,1\}^{m}=\{s^{(1)},s^{(2)},\dots,s^{(m)}\}
, denote as
\Omega_{\vec{s}}
the event where
z^{(i)}\in\{z_{j}^{(i)}\}_{j\in[k]}
if
s^{(i)}=1
. Additionally, require that
Q^{\prime}/N>1-N^{-1/3}
for all circuits, which introduces a TVD of
mN^{-1/3}
from the actual distribution. Therefore, the sampling process is as follows: with probability
\Pr[\Omega_{\vec{s}}\big|\forall i\in[m],\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=1]
, set
p^{(i)}=\frac{Q}{N(1-N^{-1/3})}
with
Q\sim\Gamma(1+s^{(i)},1)
.

Bitstring probability of a randomly chosen sample

Since
p=\frac{Q}{N(1-N^{-1/3})}
either has
Q\sim\Gamma(1,1)
(shape 1 samples) or
Q\sim\Gamma(2,1)
(shape 2 samples), we need the probability that a randomly selected sample is a shape 1 sample or a shape 2 sample.

\displaystyle q\equiv\Pr[Q\sim\Gamma(2,1)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{\vec{s}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}w(\vec{s})\Pr[\Omega_{\vec{s}}\big|\forall i\in[m],\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=1]

(IV.34)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{\vec{s}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}w(\vec{s})\frac{\Pr[\Omega_{\vec{s}}]}{1-mk^{2}/N}

(IV.35)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-k^{2}/N}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{b\in[m]}\Pr[z^{(b)}\in\{z^{(b)}_{c}\}_{c\in[k]}],

(IV.36)

where
w(\vec{s})
denotes the Hamming weight of the bitstring. The first inequality uses the fact that

\Pr[\Omega_{\vec{s}}\big|\forall i\in[m],\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=1]\leq\Pr[\Omega_{\vec{s}}]/\Pr[\forall i\in[m],\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=1]\leq\Pr[\Omega_{\vec{s}}]/(1-mk^{2}/N).

(IV.37)

For the final output, we need to incorporate the TVD distance of the distribution as well as the cap to
p_{\rm max}
. The cutoff is obviously accomplished by setting the CDF to 1 for
p>p_{\rm max}
. For the most dominant distribution with TVD equal to
mN^{-1/3}+mk^{2}/N
(the
mk^{2}/N
contribution comes from the upper bound on the probability that
\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}\neq 1
for some
i
), any probability mass under
p_{\rm min}
are moved to
p_{\rm max}
. Here,
p_{\rm min}
is chosen such that the original probability of
p<p_{\rm min}
is
mN^{-1/3}+mk^{2}/N
. This has the effect of setting the CDF to zero for
p<p_{\rm min}
and shift the CDF down by
mN^{-1/3}+mk^{2}/N
between
p_{\rm min}
and
p_{\rm max}
. Combined with Lemma
34
, this completes the proof.

∎

Now that we have a dominant distribution of the output of
\mathcal{F}
and we know the distance between
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{F}
, we can write down a dominant distribution for
\mathcal{A}
. Combining Lemma
32
and Lemma
35
and moving the additional bottom probability mass of
m\varepsilon^{\prime}
to
p_{\rm max}
in a similar way as Lemma
35
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 36
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be any algorithm making
(T,m)
-queries to
\vec{U}\sim\mathrm{Haar}^{\otimes m}
. Let
p
be the bitstring probability of a randomly chosen bitstring. If
n\geq 50,k\leq N^{-1/2},m,n\leq 10^{6}
, and

H(\vec{Z}|\vec{U}E)_{\Psi}\leq h,

(IV.38)

then
p
is dominated by a random variable with the following cumulative density function:

F(p)=\begin{cases}0&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }0\leq p\leq p_{\rm min}\\
(1-(1+qN(1-N^{-1/3})p)e^{-N(1-N^{-1/3})p})-md_{\rm Q}&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }p_{\rm min}<p<p_{\rm max}\\
1&\mathrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

(IV.39)

where

\displaystyle d_{\rm Q}

\displaystyle=N^{-1/3}+(k+\ell)^{2}/N+\varepsilon^{\prime}

(IV.40)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-(k+\ell)^{2}/N}\left(\frac{h+2}{mn}+C\right)

(IV.41)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(1-(1+qNp_{\rm min})e^{-Np_{\rm min}})-md_{\rm Q}.

(IV.42)

State of the art classical simulation algorithms that spoofs the cross entropy benchmarking (XEB) test for RCS rely on tensor networks
Pan et al. 2022
;
Kalachev et al. 2021b
;
Kalachev et al. 2021a
;
Zhao et al. 2024
;
Fu et al. 2024
;
Morvan et al. 2024
;
Liu et al. 2025
;
DeCross et al. 2025
. In the case of frugal rejection sampling
Markov et al. 2018
, the output bitstring probability
p
behaves very similarly to honest finite fidelity quantum sampling
Liu et al. 2025
, which we provide more analysis in Section
VIII.3
. For the more recent algorithm that uses post-processing by choosing the largest probability bitstring out of many bitstrings
Zhao et al. 2024
;
Fu et al. 2024
, we later show in Section
VIII.4
that when the effective fidelity is low,
p
also behaves very similarly to honest finite fidelity quantum sampling. Overall, this motivates us to approximate the behavior of classical algorithms with that of honest finite fidelity quantum sampling from a depolarized state. Concretely, one can characterize the ‘effective fidelity’ of the algorithm as well as the TVD between the actual distribution and that of honest finite fidelity quantum sampling, either theoretically or empirically.

Lemma 37
.

Consider any classical sampling process whose bitstring probability distribution for the
i
th circuit conditioned on arbitrary side information, for all
i\in[m]
, is at most
d_{\rm C}
from the distribution with the following cumulative density function:

F(x)=1-(1+\phi_{i}N(1-N^{-1/3})x)e^{-N(1-N^{-1/3})x}.

(IV.43)

If
\sum_{i\in[m]}[\phi_{i}]=\Phi_{\rm C}
and
p
is the bitstring probability of a randomly selected output string, then
p
is dominated by a random variable with the following cumulative density function:

F(p)=\begin{cases}0&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }0\leq p\leq p_{\rm min}\\
1-(1+\frac{\Phi_{\rm C}}{m}N(1-N^{-1/3})p)e^{-N(1-N^{-1/3})p}-md_{\rm C}&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }p_{\rm min}<p<p_{\rm max}\\
1&\mathrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

(IV.44)

where

\displaystyle 0=1-(1+\frac{\Phi_{\rm C}}{m}Np_{\rm min})e^{-Np_{\rm min}}-md_{\rm C}.

(IV.45)

Proof.

Consider the distribution of
p^{\prime}
which is the bitstring probability of a randomly selected output string out of
m
circuits, where each circuit samples from Eq.
IV.43
. The overall CDF is a weighted sum of Eq.
IV.43
, given by

F^{\prime}(p^{\prime})=1-(1+\frac{\Phi_{\rm C}}{m}N(1-N^{-1/3})p^{\prime})e^{-N(1-N^{-1/3})p^{\prime}}.

(IV.46)

However,
p
is sampled from bitstring probabilities with distance
d_{\rm C}
from Eq.
IV.43
, so the total distance is bounded by
md_{\rm C}
. The most dominant distribution that is
md_{\rm C}
from Eq.
IV.46
shifts the CDF down by
md_{\rm C}
within
[p_{\rm min},p_{\rm max}]
, which completes the proof.
∎

We are now finally ready to consider a mixture of classical and quantum oracle access adversary.

Lemma 38
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be any algorithm making
(T,m_{\mathrm{Q}})
-queries to
m_{\mathrm{Q}}
Haar random independent unitaries producing
m_{\mathrm{Q}}
bitstrings. Let the rest
m_{\mathrm{C}}=m-m_{\mathrm{Q}}
bitstrings be produced by a classical simulation algorithm satisfying the conditions in Lemma
37
and that
\sum_{i\in[m_{\mathrm{C}}]}\phi_{i}=\Phi_{\rm C}
. If
n\geq 50,k\leq N^{-1/2},m,n\leq 10^{6}
, and

H(\vec{Z}|\vec{U}E)_{\Psi}\leq h,

(IV.47)

then
p
is dominated by a random variable with the following cumulative density function:

F(p)=\begin{cases}0&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }0\leq p\leq p_{\rm min}\\
\left[1-\left(1+\Phi N(1-N^{-1/3})p\right)e^{-N(1-N^{-1/3})p}\right]-m\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C})&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }p_{\rm min}<p<p_{\rm max}\\
1&\mathrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

(IV.48)

where

\displaystyle d_{\rm Q}

\displaystyle=N^{-1/3}+(k+\ell)^{2}/N+\varepsilon^{\prime}

(IV.49)

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-(k+\ell)^{2}/N}\left(\frac{h+2}{mn}+C\right)+\frac{\Phi_{\rm C}}{m}

(IV.50)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(1-(1+\Phi Np_{\rm min})e^{-Np_{\rm min}})-m\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C}).

(IV.51)

Proof.

We know the
m_{\mathrm{Q}}
quantum samples have entropy at most
h
. The resulting cumulative density function of the mixture is a weighted sum of the cumulative density functions given by Lemma
36
and Lemma
37
, with
m
set to
m_{\mathrm{Q}}
and
m_{\mathrm{C}}
, respectively. The ‘middle’ part of the cumulative density function is given by

\displaystyle F(q)=

\displaystyle\left[1-\left(1+\frac{m_{\mathrm{Q}}q+m_{\mathrm{C}}\phi}{m}N(1-N^{-1/3})p\right)e^{-N(1-N^{-1/3})p}\right]-m_{\mathrm{Q}}d_{\rm Q}+m_{\mathrm{C}}d_{\rm C}

(IV.52)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left[1-\left(1+\frac{m_{\mathrm{Q}}q+m_{\mathrm{C}}\phi}{m}N(1-N^{-1/3})p\right)e^{-N(1-N^{-1/3})p}\right]-m\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C}),

(IV.53)

where

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-(k+\ell)^{2}/N}\left(\frac{h+2}{m_{\mathrm{Q}}n}+C\right)

(IV.54)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m_{\mathrm{C}}}\sum_{i\in[m_{\mathrm{C}}]}\phi_{i}=\frac{1}{m_{\mathrm{C}}}\Phi_{\rm C}.

(IV.55)

Since

m_{\mathrm{Q}}q+m_{\mathrm{C}}\phi=\frac{1}{1-(k+\ell)^{2}/N}\left(\frac{h+2}{n}+m_{\mathrm{Q}}C\right)+\Phi_{\rm C}\leq\frac{1}{1-(k+\ell)^{2}/N}\left(\frac{h+2}{n}+mC\right)+\Phi_{\rm C},

(IV.56)

we have

F(q)\geq\left[1-\left(1+\Phi N(1-N^{-1/3})p\right)e^{-N(1-N^{-1/3})p}\right]-m\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C}).

(IV.57)

With this middle part, we then need to appropriately adjust
p_{\rm min}
as required.
∎

We can additionally show that samples with the above CDF allows for an interpretation of sampling shape 2 samples with probability
\Phi
and shape 1 samples with probability
1-\Phi
, modulo a small shift.

Lemma 39
.

A random variable with the following cumulative density function:

F(x)=\begin{cases}0&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }0\leq x\leq x_{\rm min}\\
\left[1-\left(1+\Phi x\right)e^{-x}\right]-d&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }x_{\rm min}<x<x_{\rm max}\\
1&\mathrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

(IV.58)

where

\displaystyle 0=\left[1-\left(1+\Phi x_{\rm min}\right)e^{-x_{\rm min}}\right]-d,

(IV.59)

is dominated by a random variable with the following cumulative density function:

F(x)=\begin{cases}0&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }0\leq x\leq x_{\rm min}\\
(1-d)\left\{1-e^{-(x-x_{\rm min})}\left[1+\Phi(x-x_{\rm min})\right]\right\}&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }x_{\rm min}<x<x_{\rm max}\\
(1-d)\left\{1-e^{-(x-x_{\rm min})}\left[1+\Phi(x-x_{\rm min})\right]\right\}+d&\mathrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(IV.60)

Proof.

For
x_{\rm min}<x<x_{\rm max}
,

\displaystyle F(x)

\displaystyle=1-e^{-x}(1+\Phi x)-d

(IV.61)

\displaystyle=(1-d)\left[1-\frac{1}{1-d}e^{-x}(1+\Phi x)\right]

(IV.62)

\displaystyle=(1-d)\left[1-\frac{e^{x_{\rm min}}}{1+\Phi x_{\rm min}}e^{-x}(1+\Phi x)\right]

(IV.63)

\displaystyle=(1-d)\left\{1-\frac{1}{1+\Phi x_{\rm min}}e^{-(x-x_{\rm min})}\left[1+\Phi(x-x_{\rm min})+\Phi x_{\rm min}\right]\right\}

(IV.64)

\displaystyle=(1-d)\left\{1-e^{-(x-x_{\rm min})}\left[1+\frac{\Phi}{1+\Phi x_{\rm min}}(x-x_{\rm min})\right]\right\}

(IV.65)

\displaystyle\geq(1-d)\left\{1-e^{-(x-x_{\rm min})}\left[1+\Phi(x-x_{\rm min})\right]\right\},

(IV.66)

where the third equality uses the definition of
x_{\rm min}
which requires that
1-e^{-x_{\rm min}}(1+\Phi)=d
and
\frac{1}{1-d}=\frac{e^{x_{\rm min}}}{1+\Phi x_{\rm min}}
. For
x\geq x_{\rm max}
, the new cumulative density function is also always smaller than 1.
∎

IV.3
Multi-round results

With the single round statistical properties fully characterized, we can obtain a bound on the multi-round entropy. In this analysis, we will still use the entropy accumulation theorem, although statistical testing is handled separately. We first reproduce a version of entropy accumulation without statistical testing in the notation of Ref.
Dupuis et al. 2020
. We high light the following notation. Consider a register for the
i
th round
A_{i}
, denote the collection of registers
A_{i}A_{i+1}\dots A_{j}=A_{i}^{j}
and
A_{1}^{j}=A^{j}
.

Theorem 40

(Corollary 4.9 of Ref.
Dupuis et al. 2020
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{M}_{n}
and
\rho_{A_{1}^{n}B_{1}^{n}E}
be such that Eq. 26 and 27 of Ref.
Dupuis et al. 2020
hold. Then

H_{\rm min}^{\varepsilon}(A_{1}^{n}|B_{1}^{n}E)_{\rho}>\sum_{i=1}^{n}\underset{\omega_{R_{i-1}R}}{\inf}H(A_{i}|B_{i}R)_{(\mathcal{M}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{R})(\omega_{R_{i-1}R})}-c\sqrt{n},

(IV.67)

where
c=3\log_{2}(1+2d_{A})\sqrt{1-2\log_{2}\varepsilon}
.

With this theorem, if the entropy of the multi-round protocol is less than some value, then we can upper bound the sum of single round entropy. Since query access quantum devices are close to sample access quantum devices, an upper bounded entropy allows us to limit the possible probability distribution of the output bitstring probability amplitudes. We note that the above theorem results in an estimate of the smooth min-entropy of the unconditional state
\rho
. Techniques in
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
;
Dupuis et al. 2020
can be used to convert the smooth min-entropy below for
\rho
into the smooth min-entropy for
\rho|\Omega
.

Theorem 41
.

Consider an adversary with access to a quantum computer given at most
m
query access to each challenge circuit and a classical computer satisfying Lemma
37
. If
n\geq 50,k\leq N^{-1/2},m,n\leq 10^{6}
, then for any
k,\ell,\epsilon_{1}
, and
\epsilon_{2}
, either the smooth min-entropy is bounded by

H_{\rm min}^{\varepsilon}(\vec{Z}^{L}|\vec{U}^{L}E)_{\rho}>H,

(IV.68)

or the protocol of Fig.
6
aborts with probability at least

1=\Pr[\Omega]\geq 1-(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}),

(IV.69)

where

\displaystyle\epsilon_{3}=1-\sum_{i=0}^{L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max}}\tilde{\Gamma}(L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max}+i,\chi)\cdot\Pr[n_{2}=i|n_{\rm max}=n_{\rm max,max},N_{2}=N_{2,\max}],

(IV.70)

\displaystyle\Pr[n_{2}=i|n_{\rm max,max}=n_{\rm max,max},N_{2}=N_{2,\max}]=\left[\binom{L-n_{\rm max,max}}{L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max}}^{-1}{\binom{N_{2,\rm max}}{i}\binom{L-n_{\rm max,max}-N_{2,\rm max}}{L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max}-i}}\right],

(IV.71)

\displaystyle n_{\rm max,max}=\min\left(L_{\rm val},\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{3}{L_{\rm val}d}\ln\frac{1}{\epsilon_{1}}}\right)L_{\rm val}d\right),

(IV.72)

\displaystyle N_{2,\rm max}=\min\left(L-n_{\rm max,max},\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{3}{L\Phi}\ln\frac{1}{\epsilon_{2}}}\right)L\Phi\right),

(IV.73)

\displaystyle\chi=(L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max})\left(s^{*}-\frac{N}{L_{\rm val}}(n_{\rm max,max}p_{\rm max}+(L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max})p_{\rm min})\right)(1-N^{-1/3}),

(IV.74)

\displaystyle d=m\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C}),

(IV.75)

\displaystyle 0=1-e^{-x_{\rm min}}(1+x_{\rm min})-d,

(IV.76)

\displaystyle p_{\rm min}=\frac{x_{\rm min}}{N(1-N^{-1/3})},

(IV.77)

\displaystyle d_{\rm Q}=N^{-1/3}+(k+\ell)^{2}/N+\varepsilon^{\prime},

(IV.78)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\prime}=T\left(\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k}\right)^{k}\right)}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}\right),

(IV.79)

\displaystyle\Phi=\frac{\Phi_{\rm C}}{m}+\frac{1}{1-(k+\ell)^{2}/N}\left(\frac{2}{mn}+\phi C+\frac{1}{Lmn}(H+c\sqrt{L})\right),

(IV.80)

\displaystyle C=\frac{1}{n}\left(\log_{2}n+\log_{2}(k+\ell)+3\right)+4m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)+1.001\times\frac{16(k+\ell)^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}+0.001,

(IV.81)

\displaystyle c=3\log_{2}(1+2\times 2^{mn})\sqrt{1-2\log_{2}\varepsilon}.

(IV.82)

Proof.

For a single round, we consider the cumulative density function given by Lemma
38
, relabeling
\Phi,h
as
\Phi_{a},h_{a}
for the
a
th round. Applying Lemma
39
with rescaling of
x\rightarrow N(1-N^{-1/3})p
and using the observation from Lemma
34
, the dominant cumulative density function can be described by the following sampling process of
p
:

1.

With probability
d=m\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C})
, set
p=p_{\rm max}
.

2.

With probability
(1-d)(1-\Phi_{a})
, set
p=\frac{Q}{N(1-N^{-1/3})}+p_{\rm min}
with
Q\sim\Gamma(1,1)
.

3.

Otherwise, set
p=\frac{Q}{N(1-N^{-1/3})}+p_{\rm min}
with
Q\sim\Gamma(2,1)
.

Here, all rounds are considered to have the same
p_{\rm min}
, which is taken as the maximum possible value attainable when
\Phi_{a}=1
, which is given by the conditions of this theorem.

We obtain an upper bound on the sum of
\Phi_{a}
, given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\sum_{a}\Phi_{a}

\displaystyle\leq\left[\frac{\Phi_{\rm C}}{m}+\frac{1}{1-(k+\ell)^{2}/N}\left(\frac{h_{a}+2}{mn}+C\right)\right]

(IV.83)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Phi_{\rm C}}{m}+\frac{1}{1-(k+\ell)^{2}/N}\left(\frac{2}{mn}+C+\frac{1}{Lmn}\sum_{a}h_{a}\right)

(IV.84)

If
H_{\rm min}^{\varepsilon}(\vec{Z}^{L}|\vec{C}^{L}E)_{\rho}<H
, Theorem
40
implies

\sum_{a\in[L]}h_{a}=\sum_{a\in[L]}\underset{\omega_{R_{a-1}R}}{\inf}H(\vec{Z}_{a}|\vec{C}_{a}R)_{(\mathcal{M}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{R})(\omega_{R_{a-1}R})}<H+c\sqrt{L},

(IV.85)

and therefore

\frac{1}{L}\sum_{a}\Phi_{a}<\frac{\Phi_{\rm C}}{m}+\frac{1}{1-(k+\ell)^{2}/N}\left(\frac{2}{mn}+C+\frac{1}{Lmn}(H+c\sqrt{L})\right)\equiv\Phi.

(IV.86)

For validation set
\mathcal{V}
of size
L_{\rm val}
, we can upper bound the probability of obtaining
n_{\rm max}
validation samples of
p_{\rm max}
. Since every sample has probability
d
of being
p_{\rm max}
and are independent,
n_{\rm max}
is given by the binomial distribution, and the tail bound can be obtained using the known Chernoff bound for sum of Poisson trials (Theorem 4.4 of Ref.
Mitzenmacher and Upfal 2005
):

\displaystyle\Pr[n_{\rm max}\geq(1+\delta)L_{\rm val}d]

\displaystyle<e^{-\frac{\delta^{2}L_{\rm val}d}{3}}\leq\epsilon_{1}

(IV.87)

\displaystyle\Pr[n_{\rm max}\geq n_{\rm max,max}]

\displaystyle<\epsilon_{1},\quad n_{\rm max,max}=\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{3}{L_{\rm val}d}\ln\frac{1}{\epsilon_{1}}}\right)L_{\rm val}d.

(IV.88)

Similarly, we can bound the number
n_{2}
of shape
2
samples in the validation set. We can even consider the worse case where each sample is shape 2 with probability
\Phi_{a}
instead of
(1-d)\Phi_{a}
. We can first bound the number
N_{2}
of shape 2 samples in total, which is given by the same Chernoff bound above:

\displaystyle\Pr[N_{2}\geq N_{2,\max}]

\displaystyle<\epsilon_{2},\quad N_{2,\max}=\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{3}{\sum_{a\in[L]}\Phi_{a}}\ln\frac{1}{\epsilon_{2}}}\right)\sum_{a\in[L]}\Phi_{a}=\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{3}{L\Phi}\ln\frac{1}{\epsilon_{2}}}\right)L\Phi.

(IV.89)

Since the client performs verification only on a much smaller set of circuits
\mathcal{V}
, the distribution of the score over the verification set depends on the number
n_{2}
of shape 2 samples in the verification set, not the number
N_{2}
of shape 2 samples in total. Given
n_{\rm max}
samples are set to
p_{\rm max}
, each of the remaining
L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max}
verification samples is drawn from a total of
L-n_{\rm max}
samples without replacement. If
N_{2}
out of
M
samples are shape 2, then the probability that
n_{2}
out of
L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max}
of the remaining validation samples are shape 2 is given precisely by the hypergeometric distribution
\text{Hypergeometric}(L-n_{\rm max},N_{2},L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max})
.
Rice 2007
.

Given
n_{2}
shape 2 samples and
n_{\rm max}
samples set to
p_{\rm max}
in the validation set, there should be
n_{1}=L_{\rm val}-n_{2}-n_{\rm max}
shape 1 samples in the validation set. The sum of all the
Q
’s is distributed according to
\Gamma(n_{1}+2n_{2},1)
. Further, the contribution to the final score due to shape 1 and shape 2 samples is scaled by
(1-N^{-1/3})^{-1}
. The
n_{\rm max}
samples set to
p_{\rm max}
increases the score by
\frac{n_{\rm max}Np_{\rm max}}{L_{\rm val}}
. The offset in
Q
by
x_{\rm min}
for all shape 1 and shape 2 samples contributes
\frac{(n_{1}+n_{2})Np_{\rm min}}{L_{\rm val}}
to the score. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pr[s\leq s^{*}\Big|n_{\rm max}=n_{\rm max,max}\land N_{2}=N_{2,\max}]

(IV.90)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max}}\Pr[\text{Sum of all $Q$'s}\leq\chi|n_{\rm max}=n_{\rm max,max},n_{2}=i]\cdot\Pr[n_{2}=i|n_{\rm max}=n_{\rm max,max},N_{2}=N_{2,\max}]

(IV.91)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max}}\tilde{\Gamma}(L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max}+i,\chi)\cdot\Pr[n_{2}=i|n_{\rm max}=n_{\rm max,max},N_{2}=N_{2,\max}]=1-\epsilon_{3},

(IV.92)

where

\displaystyle s^{*}=\frac{1}{L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max}}\chi(1-N^{-1/3})^{-1}+\frac{N}{L_{\rm val}}(n_{\rm max,max}p_{\rm max}+(L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max})p_{\rm min}),

(IV.93)

\displaystyle\chi=(L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max})\left(s^{*}-\frac{N}{L_{\rm val}}(n_{\rm max,max}p_{\rm max}+(L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max})p_{\rm min})\right)(1-N^{-1/3}),

(IV.94)

\displaystyle\Pr[n_{2}=i|n_{\rm max,max}=n_{\rm max,max},N_{2}=N_{2,\max}]=\left[\binom{L-n_{\rm max,max}}{L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max}}^{-1}{\binom{N_{2,\rm max}}{i}\binom{L-n_{\rm max,max}-N_{2,\rm max}}{L_{\rm val}-n_{\rm max,max}-i}}\right].

(IV.95)

Putting it all together, the probability that the adversary exceeds threshold
\chi
is

\displaystyle 1=\Pr[\Omega]=

\displaystyle\Pr[s\geq s^{*}]

(IV.96)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Pr[n_{\rm max}>n_{\rm max,max}\lor N_{2}>N_{2,\max}]

(IV.97)

\displaystyle+\Pr[n_{\rm max}\leq n_{\rm max,max}\land N_{2}\leq N_{2,\max}]\Pr[s\geq\chi|n_{\rm max}\leq n_{\rm max,max}\land N_{2}\leq N_{2,\max}]

(IV.98)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Pr[n_{\rm max}>n_{\rm max,max}\lor N_{2}>N_{2,\max}]+\Pr[s\geq\chi|n_{\rm max}=n_{\rm max,max}\land N_{2}=N_{2,\max}]

(IV.99)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}.

(IV.100)

This concludes the analysis when we have quantum device fidelity
\phi=1
.
∎

Remark 42
.

We can also consider noisy adversaries. Now, consider a noisy quantum device whose output
\vec{Z}=\vec{z}
follows the probability distribution
\rho_{\rm noisy}(\vec{z})=\phi\rho_{\rm ideal}(\vec{z})+(1-\phi)\sigma(\vec{z})
, where
\rho_{\rm ideal}
is the probability distribution of an ideal quantum device and
\sigma
is some other normalized probability distribution. Further, assume that
\sigma
is not usefully dependent on the input quantum circuit. If the noisy adversary solves
(1+\delta)-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
, then the ideal algorithm should be able to solve
(1+\delta/\phi)-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
, which means the ideal algorithm would have entropy
h(\delta/\phi)
. The noisy algorithm entropy can be lower bounded by
\phi h(\delta/\phi)
. This allows us to restate a noisy version of Lemma
32
and Theorem
41
with

C\rightarrow\phi C.

(IV.101)

To see why, we first claim that
C\rightarrow\phi C
of Lemma
32
has the above form. If for the noisy algorithm,
H(\vec{Z}|\vec{U}E)_{\rho}\leq h
, then the worse case entropy bound for the ideal algorithm is
H(\vec{Z}|\vec{U}E)_{\Psi}\leq h/\phi
. Now, for the ideal algorithm we have
\delta=(h/\phi+2)/(mn)+C
, and the noisy algorithm multiplies
\delta
by
\phi
, yielding
\delta=(h+2\phi)/mn+\phi C\leq(h+2)/mn+\phi C
.

Remark 43
.

Here, we suffer from the
\sqrt{L}
penalty in
\Phi
, which is a consequence of bounding the multi-round smooth min-entropy using EAT. However, one can obtain a better bound using Eq. 5 of Ref.
Tomamichel et al. 2009
. This would require obtaining single-round collision entropy bound for our certified randomness protocol. Additionally, if we only assume an independent but not i.i.d. adversary, then the asymptotic equipartition theorem requires generalization.

One can also potentially resolve the non-i.i.d. issue by “postselecting” on having
Q
total samples returned from one of the
k
samples provided to the simulation algorithm
\mathcal{F}
. This would allow a similar analysis to
Liu et al. 2025
to be conducted.

We can also devise a further improved entropy estimate using the improved single-round entropy bound without the
\log_{2}(k+\ell)
penalty that is valid in the
T=1
,
m=1
setting.

Theorem 44
.

For the special case of
T=1
and
m=1
, Theorem
41
holds with modified parameters

\displaystyle C=\frac{1}{n}\left(\log_{2}n+3\right)+4\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)+1.001\times\frac{16\ln^{2}N}{N}+0.001,

(IV.102)

where
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
is as defined in Corollary
27
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to Theorem
41
, except we change the proof of Lemma
30
. Instead of using Theorem
46
, we use Theorem
48
with the improve entropy. This improves the bound on
\delta
for Lemma
30
.
∎

V
Multi-round security under adaptive hybrid adversaries

V.1
Direct application of entropy accumulation

To be secure against the most general adaptive adversaries, analyses are typically carried out under the framework of entropy accumulation, and each round is tested independently with some fixed probability. We define a multi-round protocol suitable for entropy accumulation analysis in Figure
7
. Consider the situation where each batch has validation probability
\gamma
, and to validate a batch means choosing a random circuit in the batch to validate. To analyze the protocol, we use an entropy accumulation theorem, originally proposed by
Dupuis et al. 2020
. Specifically, we consider the improved version described in
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
, and further improve the constant correction term, which we describe below.

Protocol Arguments:

\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Number of qubits}

\displaystyle m\in\mathbb{N}\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Number of circuits per round (batch size)}

\displaystyle L\in\mathbb{N}\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Number of rounds}

\displaystyle T_{\rm batch}\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Threshold on the time allowed for the samples to return}

\displaystyle p_{\rm max}\in(1/2^{n},1]\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Maximum contribution probability}

\displaystyle s^{*}\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Threshold for test score}

\displaystyle\gamma\penalty\

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \text{Probability of verifying a round}

The Protocol:

1.

Set
s=0
.

2.

For
i\in[L]
, do:

(a)

Sample
m
Haar random unitaries.

(b)

Send them to the server and start the timer.

(c)

If does not receive the bitstrings in time
T_{\rm batch}
, abort. Otherwise, store the bitstrings
\vec{Z}_{i}
.

(d)

Sample
T_{i}\sim\mathsf{Bernoulli}(\gamma)
.

(e)

If
T_{i}=1
, sample a circuit uniform from the batch of
m
circuits for the
i
th round and add it to the validation set.

3.

For each
T_{i}=1
, compute the bitstring probability
p
for the chosen validation circuit of that batch and add
\frac{N\min(p,p_{\rm max})}{\gamma L}
to
s
. Finally, abort if
s<s^{*}
.

Figure 7:
Description of the certified randomness protocol.

In this portion of the text, we use the notation of Section V of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
. We first provide an improved version of the entropy accumulation theorem.

Theorem 45

(Theorem V.2 of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
, improved)
.

For
w=2\log_{2}d_{A}+\max(f)-\min_{\Sigma}(f)
, if
w\geq 3
and
n\geq\frac{8\ln 2\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}\Pr[\Omega]^{2}}}{V^{2}}
, then the smooth min-entropy conditioned on the event of not aborting (
\Omega
) is

H_{\rm min}^{\varepsilon}(A^{n}_{1}|B^{n}_{1}E)_{\rho|\Omega}>nh-c\sqrt{n}-c^{\prime},

(V.1)

where

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=V\sqrt{(2\ln 2)\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}\Pr[\Omega]^{2}}}

(V.2)

\displaystyle c^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}\Pr[\Omega]^{2}}}{V^{2}\ln 2}K

(V.3)

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=4\times(w+1)^{3}\times 2^{\frac{w\sqrt{2\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}\Pr[\Omega]^{2}}}}{V\sqrt{n\ln 2}}},

(V.4)

and

V=\sqrt{\mathrm{var}(f)+2}+\log_{2}(2d_{A}^{2}+1)

(V.5)

if
A
is quantum, or

V=\sqrt{\mathrm{var}(f)+2}+\log_{2}(2d_{A}+1)

(V.6)

if
A
is classical.

Proof.

Eq. 29 of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
is

H_{\rm min}^{\varepsilon}(A^{n}_{1}|B^{n}_{1}E)_{\rho|\Omega}\geq nh-n\frac{(\alpha-1)\ln 2}{2}V^{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}\Pr[\Omega]^{2}}-n(\alpha-1)^{2}K_{\alpha},

(V.7)

where
K_{\alpha}
is as in Eq. 31 of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
. Note that we do not bound
K_{\alpha}
with
K
as in
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
, since the effect of alpha optimization will be important here. We also note that the value of
V
here arises due to the invocation of Corollary IV.2 in Eq. 35 of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
, and the discrepancy between quantum and classical
A
can be traced back to Eq. 37 and understood with Corollary III.5 of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
.
Now, we optimize
\alpha
. Choose

\alpha=1+\frac{\sqrt{2\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}\Pr[\Omega]^{2}}}}{V\sqrt{n\ln 2}}.

(V.8)

The assumptions on
n
and
w
of the theorems means that this choice of
\alpha
satisfies
\alpha\leq 1+\frac{1}{2\ln 2}
, and

K_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{6\cdot(2-\alpha)^{3}\ln 2}2^{\frac{w\sqrt{2\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}\Pr[\Omega]^{2}}}}{V\sqrt{n\ln 2}}}\ln^{3}(2^{w}+e^{2}).

(V.9)

Further, with the aforementioned lower bound on
n
, the upper bound on
\alpha
, and the lower bound on
w
, we have

K_{\alpha}\leq\frac{1}{6\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2\ln 2})^{3}\ln 2}\ln^{3}(2^{w+1})2^{\frac{w\sqrt{2\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}\Pr[\Omega]^{2}}}}{V\sqrt{n\ln 2}}}\leq 4\times(w+1)^{3}\times 2^{\frac{w\sqrt{2\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}\Pr[\Omega]^{2}}}}{V\sqrt{n\ln 2}}}.

(V.10)

Finally, substituting
\alpha
into the expression of the smooth min-entropy completes the proof.
∎

This results in the following theorem.

Theorem 46
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{L}
be
(T,m)
-query sequential processes given access to the first system of a bipartite state
\rho_{DE}
and outputting
\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{L}
. If
n\geq 50
,
k\leq N^{-1/2}
,
m,n\leq 10^{6}
, and the protocol in Figure
7
does not abort, then

H_{\rm min}^{\varepsilon}(\vec{Z}^{L}|\vec{U}^{L}E)_{\mathcal{M}_{L}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{M}_{1}(\rho)|\Omega}\geq Lh(s^{*})-\sqrt{L}c-c^{\prime},

(V.11)

where
c,c^{\prime},V
follow the definitions in Theorem
45
in the case of classical
A
,

\displaystyle h(s)

\displaystyle=m\left[\left(s-1-F_{C}-2m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)-1.001\frac{16(k+\ell)^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}-0.001\right)n-\log_{2}n-\log_{2}(k+\ell)-5\right]-2

(V.12)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=2mn+h(Np_{\rm max})-h(0)

(V.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}(f)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\gamma}(h(Np_{\rm max})-h(0))^{2},

(V.14)

and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is as defined in Theorem
25
.

Proof.

Consider a quantum channel that takes as input the bitstring for the
i
th round on register
\vec{Z}_{i}
and outputs a test output on register
X_{i}
. Specifically, the domain of
X_{i}
is
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\{\perp\}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=[0,Np_{\rm max}]
. With probability
\gamma
, the channel output a test score
X_{i}\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
; with probability
1-\gamma
, the channel does not test and outputs
X_{i}=\perp
. For each test round, say the
i
th round, where the single validated circuit has bitstring probability
p
, the test output is the score
N\min(p,p_{\rm max})
.

For entropy accumulation, we use Theorem
45
, and define the min-tradeoff function as Lemma V.5 of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
. We first define
g:\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})
is the set of all probability distributions over
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
.

g(q^{\prime})=m\left[\left(s(q^{\prime})-1-F_{C}-2m\varepsilon^{\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)-1.001\frac{16(k+\ell)^{2}\ln^{2}N}{N}-0.001\right)n-\log_{2}n-\log_{2}(k+\ell)-5\right]-2,

(V.15)

where

s(q^{\prime})=\int_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}xq^{\prime}(x)dx

(V.16)

is the average score of the distribution
q^{\prime}
, and
q^{\prime}
is the probability distribution of the test output in the case of a test round. Since having an average score of
s
implies solving
(1+s)-\mathrm{MXHOG}
, the definition of
g(q^{\prime})
immediately follows from Eq. 42 of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
and Eq.
III.95
.

Then, by Lemma V.5 of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
, the following function
f(q):\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a min-tradeoff function:

\displaystyle f(\delta_{x})

\displaystyle=\max(g)+\frac{1}{\gamma}(g(\delta_{x})-\max(g))\forall x\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}

(V.17)

\displaystyle f(\delta_{\perp})

\displaystyle=\max(g).

(V.18)

By the same lemma,

\displaystyle\max(f)=

\displaystyle\max(g)

(V.19)

\displaystyle\min_{\Sigma}(f)\geq

\displaystyle\min(g)

(V.20)

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}(f)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma}(\max(g)-\min(g))^{2}.

(V.21)

Since
\max(g)=h(Np_{\rm max})
and
\min(g)=h(0)
, plugging everything into Theorem
45
completes the proof.
∎

We can evaluate the entropy guarantee of the protocol using Theorem
45
. Alternatively, we can use Eq.
V.7
, substitute the value of
V
as in Theorem
45
but keep the
\alpha
dependence of
K_{\alpha}
, and optimize over
\alpha
. Since the result of Theorem
45
is with a specific non-optimal value of
\alpha
, the entropy guarantee obtained by the second approach is strictly better. However, in the regime of high entropy rate, the two approaches yield similar results as the choice of
\alpha
made by Theorem
45
is nearly optimal.

Finally, we consider the improved single-round entropy without the
\log_{2}(k+\ell)
penalty for the special case of
m=1
and
T=1
. We additionally assume that the adversary’s quantum computer has fidelity
\phi_{\rm adv}
. We assume that with probability
1-\phi
, at least a single error occurs when executing the oracle of the quantum circuit. We can assume that a single error results in the output state of a completely random Haar random quantum circuit. This motivates us to define the following notion of fidelity.

Definition 47
.

The quantum device output is
\rho_{\rm noisy}(\vec{z})=\phi\rho_{\rm ideal}(\vec{z})+(1-\phi)\sigma(\vec{z})
, where
\rho_{\rm ideal}
is the probability distribution of an ideal quantum device and
\sigma
is some other normalized probability distribution independent of the quantum circuit.

Now, we can improve the entropy bound considering a finite fidelity quantum adversary.

Theorem 48
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{L}
be a fidelity
\phi_{\rm adv}
(as defined in Definition
47
)
(1,1)
-query sequential processes with given access to the first system of a bipartite state
\rho_{DE}
and outputting
\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{L}
. If
n\geq 50
,
k\leq N^{-1/2}
,
n\leq 10^{6}
, and the protocol in Figure
7
does not abort, then

H_{\rm min}^{\varepsilon}(Z^{L}|U^{L}E)_{\mathcal{M}_{L}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{M}_{1}(\rho)|\Omega}\geq Lh(s^{*})-\sqrt{L}c-c^{\prime},

(V.22)

where
c,c^{\prime},V
follow the definitions in Theorem
45
in the case of classical
A
, and

\displaystyle h(s)

\displaystyle=\phi_{\rm adv}\left[\left(\frac{s-1-F_{C}}{\phi_{\rm adv}}-2\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)-1.001\frac{16\ln^{2}N}{N}-0.001\right)n-\log_{2}n-7\right]

(V.23)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=2n+h(Np_{\rm max})-h(0)

(V.24)

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}(f)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\gamma}(h(Np_{\rm max})-h(0))^{2},

(V.25)

where
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
is as defined in Corollary
27
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same structure as Theorem
46
with the appropriate new single-round bound due to Corollary
27
. The effect of finite fidelity is to rescale the score of
\rho_{\rm ideal}
since the ideal state must do better on the test score for the average score to be
s
. For a mixture of
m^{\prime}
classical samples per round and
m^{\prime\prime}=m-m^{\prime}
quantum samples where the quantum computer is ideal, the quantum algorithm must solve
(1+\delta^{\prime\prime})-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m^{\prime\prime}}
with

\delta^{\prime\prime}=\frac{1}{1-m^{\prime}/m}\left(\delta-\frac{m^{\prime}}{m}\delta^{\prime}\right)=\frac{\delta-F_{\rm C}}{1-m^{\prime}/m}

(V.26)

as discussed in Eq.
III.94
. For a quantum device with fidelity
\phi_{\rm adv}
, we have
\delta^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\delta^{\prime\prime}/\phi_{\rm adv}
.
The entropy is scaled by
\phi_{\rm adv}
since we assume
\sigma
may not contribute any entropy. Propagating the new
\delta^{\prime\prime}
and rescaling entropy by
\phi_{\rm adv}
completes the proof.
∎

V.2
Improving the variance

However, both approaches still do not provide very good entropy estimates. Although the dependence on the testing probability
\gamma
is improved from
Dupuis et al. 2020
to
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
, the dependence on the system size is worse. Further, the test bitstring probability range for each round is
[0,p_{\rm max}]
, while the mean value of the is much lower than
p_{\rm max}
(e.g. one can reasonably choose
p=10/2^{n}
to not lose too much of the probability mass, but the mean score is at most
2/2^{n}
). This means that the variance
\mathrm{var}(f)
is large, which results in a significant penalty.

One approach to improve the variance is to reduce
p_{\rm max}
. Naively speaking, reducing
p_{\rm max}
would cause a reduction in the XEB score in the honest case. For the malicious case, an adversary could output samples completely within
[0,p_{\rm max}]
and the XEB score may not be affected. Fortunately, this is not the case, and reducing
p_{\rm max}
must reduce the adversary’s XEB score as well. This is because the distribution of the bitstring probability
p
must be close to that of finite-fidelity honest sampling from a depolarized state as given by Lemma
38
. Overall, this allows us to reduce
p_{\rm max}
. Although the honest case XEB score would reduce, the entropy guarantee should not reduce significantly due to the inability for any adversary to do well.

Theorem 49
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be any algorithm making
(T,m_{\mathrm{Q}})
-queries to
m_{\mathrm{Q}}
Haar random independent unitaries producing
m_{\mathrm{Q}}
bitstrings. Let the rest
m_{\mathrm{Q}}=m-m_{\mathrm{Q}}
bitstrings be produced by a classical simulation algorithm satisfying the conditions in Lemma
37
and that
\sum_{i\in[m_{\mathrm{Q}}]}\phi_{i}=\Phi_{\rm C}
. If
n\geq 50
,
k\leq N^{-1/2}
,
m,n\leq 10^{6}
, and
\mathcal{A}
solves
(1+\delta)-\mathrm{MXHOG}_{m}
when the bitstring probability contributions are capped at
p_{\rm max}
, then

\displaystyle H(\vec{Z}|\vec{U}E)_{\mathcal{A}^{\vec{C}}}

\displaystyle\geq mn\left\{\left[\frac{\delta\cdot(1-N^{-1/3})-1-dx_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}+e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}}{1-e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}(1+x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}-\frac{\Phi_{\rm C}}{m}\right]\left[1-\frac{(k+\ell)^{2}}{N}\right]-C\right\}-2

(V.27)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(1-(1+Np_{\rm min})e^{-Np_{\rm min}})-m\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C}),

(V.28)

where
x_{\rm min}=N(1-N^{-1/3})p_{\rm min},x_{\rm max}=N(1-N^{-1/3})p_{\rm max}
, and
d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C},\Phi_{\rm C},C
be defined as in Lemma
38
. Note that
C
of Lemma
38
is defined in Lemma
32
.

Proof.

By the same arguments as Theorem
41
, the dominant cumulative density function of the truncated bitstring probability can be described by the following sampling process of
p
:

1.

With probability
d=m\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C})
, set
p=p_{\rm max}
.

2.

With probability
(1-d)(1-\Phi)
, set
p=\frac{Q}{N(1-N^{-1/3})}+p_{\rm min}
with
Q\sim\Gamma(1,1)
.

3.

Otherwise, set
p=\frac{Q}{N(1-N^{-1/3})}+p_{\rm min}
with
Q\sim\Gamma(2,1)
.

4.

After this, if
p>p_{\rm max}
, set
p
to
p_{\rm max}
.

Here, all rounds are considered to have the same
p_{\rm min}
, which is taken as the maximum possible value attainable when
\Phi_{a}=1
, which is given by the conditions of Theorem
41
. We have added the final truncation here. This is in contrast to Theorem
41
, where not truncating after leads to easier analysis.

The expected value of
x=N(1-N^{-1/3})p
is therefore a weighted sum of the expected value of
x
in three cases: setting
p=p_{\rm max}
directly, setting
p=\min(p_{\rm max},\frac{Q}{N(1-N^{-1/3})}+p_{\rm min})
with
Q\sim\Gamma(1,1)
, and setting
p=\min(p_{\rm max},\frac{Q}{N(1-N^{-1/3})}+p_{\rm min})
with
Q\sim\Gamma(2,1)
.

In the second case, the expected value is

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}}(Q+x_{\rm min})\mathrm{PDF}(Q)dQ+\Pr[Q>x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}]\cdot x_{\rm max}

(V.29)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}}(Q+x_{\rm min})e^{-Q}dQ+x_{\rm max}\left(1-\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}}e^{-Q}dQ\right)

(V.30)

\displaystyle=x_{\rm max}-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})[1-e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}]+[1-(1+x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}]

(V.31)

\displaystyle=1+x_{\rm min}-e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}.

(V.32)

In the third case, the expected value is

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle_{3}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}}(Q+x_{\rm min})\mathrm{PDF}(Q)dQ+\Pr[Q>x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}]\cdot x_{\rm max}

(V.33)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}}(Q+x_{\rm min})Qe^{-Q}dQ+x_{\rm max}\left(1-\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}}Qe^{-Q}dQ\right)

(V.34)

\displaystyle=x_{\rm max}-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})[1-(1+x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}]+2\cdot\left[1-\left(1+x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}+\frac{1}{2}(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})^{2}\right)e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}\right]

(V.35)

\displaystyle=2+x_{\rm min}-e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}[2+x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}].

(V.36)

Therefore, the overall mean value of
x
is

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle

\displaystyle\leq dx_{\rm max}+(1-d)(1-\Phi)\langle x\rangle_{2}+\Phi(1-d)\langle x\rangle_{3}

(V.37)

\displaystyle\leq dx_{\rm max}+(1-\Phi)\langle x\rangle_{2}+\Phi\langle x\rangle_{3}

(V.38)

\displaystyle=dx_{\rm max}+\langle x\rangle_{2}+\Phi\cdot(\langle x\rangle_{3}-\langle x\rangle_{2})

(V.39)

\displaystyle=dx_{\rm max}+1+x_{\rm min}-e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}+\Phi\cdot\left[1-e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}(1+x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})\right].

(V.40)

We then have

\delta=\frac{\langle x\rangle}{1-N^{-1/3}}-1\Rightarrow\langle x\rangle=(\delta+1)(1-N^{-1/3}),

(V.41)

which implies

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(\delta+1)\cdot(1-N^{-1/3})-1-dx_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}+e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}}{1-e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}(1+x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}.

(V.42)

Using the definition of
\Phi
in Lemma
38
allows us to lower bound
h
and complete the proof.
∎

Remark 50
.

When
p_{\rm max}
is too small, more of the tail of the distribution for large
p
is ignored, which hinders the ability to discriminate between different fidelities.

Another approach to improve
\mathrm{var}(f)
is to use the equality in Lemma V.5 of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
instead of the upper bound.

Lemma 51
.

The variance
\mathrm{var}(f)
obeys

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}(f)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}}q^{\prime}(x)(\max(g)-g(\delta_{x}))^{2}dx-(\max(g)-g(q^{\prime}))^{2}

(V.43)

\displaystyle g(q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}}q^{\prime}(x)g(\delta_{x})dx

(V.44)

\displaystyle q^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=m\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C})\cdot\delta(x)+e^{-x}\cdot I_{(0,x_{\rm max})}(x)+\left[e^{-x_{\rm max}}-m\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C})\right]\cdot\delta(x-x_{\rm max})

(V.45)

\displaystyle I_{(0,a)}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1\penalty\ \mathrm{if}\penalty\ 0<x<a\\
0\penalty\ \mathrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

(V.46)

\displaystyle d_{\rm Q}

\displaystyle=N^{-1/3}+(k+\ell)^{2}/N+\varepsilon^{\prime},

(V.47)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle=T\left(\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k}\right)^{k}\right)}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}\right).

(V.48)

Proof.

As shown in Lemma V.5 of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
,

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}q(x)\left(f(\delta_{x})-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}q(x)f(\delta_{x})\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{\gamma}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}q^{\prime}(x)((\max(g)-g(\delta_{x}))-\gamma(\max(g)-g(q^{\prime})))^{2}+(1-\gamma)(\max(g)-g(q^{\prime}))^{2},

(V.49)

and the first term is expanded as

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\frac{q^{\prime}(x)}{\gamma}(\max(g)-g(\delta_{x}))^{2}-2(\max(g)-g(q^{\prime}))^{2}+\gamma(\max(g)-g(q^{\prime}))^{2}.

(V.50)

Combining these existing results, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}q(x)\left(f(\delta_{x})-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}q(x)f(\delta_{x})\right)^{2}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\frac{q^{\prime}(x)}{\gamma}(\max(g)-g(\delta_{x}))^{2}-(\max(g)-g(q^{\prime}))^{2}.

(V.51)

Further, by Definition V.1. of Ref.
Dupuis and Fawzi 2019
,

\mathrm{var}(f)=\max_{q:\Sigma_{i}(q)\neq\emptyset}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}q(x)\left(f(\delta_{x})-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}q(x)f(\delta_{x})\right)^{2},

(V.52)

we must maximize Eq.
V.51
over all valid
q^{\prime}
. Fortunately, we know the test output probability distribution must be close to that of finite fidelity sampling. For example, see Lemma
38
. By examining Eq.
V.51
, we see that pushing as much probability mass to
x=0
maximizes the variance. However, the CDF in Lemma
38
pushes as much probability mass to
p_{\rm max}
as possible. Following the same steps that lead to Lemma
38
except pushing probability mass to low values, we get the CDF

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}(x)=\begin{cases}1-e^{-x}+m\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C})&\mathrm{if\penalty\ }0<x<x_{\rm max}\\
1&\mathrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

(V.53)

which corresponds to the PDF
q^{\prime}
as stated in the Lemma. Substituting
q^{\prime}
into Eq.
V.51
completes the proof.
∎

With the aforementioned two improvements, applying the entropy accumulation theorem results in an improved multi-round entropy guarantee.

Corollary 52
.

If the protocol in Figure
7
does not abort, then

H_{\rm min}^{\varepsilon}(\vec{Z}^{L}|\vec{U}^{L}E)_{\mathcal{M}_{L}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{M}_{1}(\rho)|\Omega}\geq Lh(s^{*})-\sqrt{L}c-c^{\prime},

(V.54)

where
c,c^{\prime},V
follow the definitions in Theorem
45
in the case of classical
A
,

\displaystyle h(s)

\displaystyle=mn\left\{\left[\frac{s\cdot(1-N^{-1/3})-1-dx_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}+e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}}{1-e^{-(x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}(1+x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}-\frac{\Phi_{\rm C}}{m}\right]\left[1-\frac{(k+\ell)^{2}}{N}\right]-C\right\}-2

(V.55)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=2mn+h(Np_{\rm max})-h(0)

(V.56)

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}(f)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}}q^{\prime}(x)(h(Np_{\rm max})-h(x))^{2}dx-(h(Np_{\rm max})-h(q^{\prime}))^{2}

(V.57)

\displaystyle h(q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}}q^{\prime}(x)h(x)dx,

(V.58)

x_{\rm min},x_{\rm max},\Phi_{\rm C}
are as defined in
49
,
q^{\prime}
is as defined in Lemma
51
, and
C
is as defined in Lemma
38
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same steps as Theorem
46
using the single round entropy bound in Theorem
49
as well as
\mathrm{var}(f)
in Lemma
51
.
∎

For the special case of
T=1,m=1
, we can use the improved single-round entropy bound.

Corollary 53
.

For
T=1,m=1
, Corollary
52
holds with
C,\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
as defined in Theorem
44
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}\rightarrow\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
.

Additionally, we can put heuristic adjustments assuming the adversary’s quantum computer has limited fidelity like before. The output probability distribution of the quantum algorithm is modified from Lemma
36
.

Corollary 54
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{L}
be a fidelity
\phi_{\rm adv}
(as defined in Definition
47
)
(T,m)
-query sequential processes given access to the first system of a bipartite state
\rho_{DE}
and outputting
\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{L}
. If
n\geq 50
,
k\leq N^{-1/2}
,
m,n\leq 10^{6}
, and the protocol in Figure
7
does not abort, then the output has the same entropy as Corollary
52
except
C\rightarrow\phi C
. For
T=1,m=1
, the output has the same entropy as Corollary
53
except
C\rightarrow\phi C
.

Proof.

We start with the difference in Lemma
36
which specifies the distribution of the quantum algorithm given an upper bound of the entropy. For the sample of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
to still have entropy
h
, the entropy of
\mathcal{A}
must be
h/\phi_{\rm adv}
. The cumulative density function should be a weighted sum of the original function with
h\rightarrow h/\phi_{\rm adv}
and
1-e^{-Np}
(corresponding to the bitstring probability of a fixed bitstring, which is the same as that of uniformly random bitstrings since it is a probability over Haar random unitaries) with weight
\phi_{\rm adv}
and
1-\phi_{\rm adv}
. The additional discrepancy is acceptable since we want a dominant function.

This difference from Lemma
36
can be propagated to Lemma
38
, resulting in

\Phi\rightarrow\frac{1}{1-(k+\ell)^{2}/N}\left(\frac{h+2\phi_{\rm adv}}{mn}+\phi_{\rm adv}C\right)+\frac{\Phi_{\rm C}}{m}.

(V.59)

Finally, propagating this into Theorem
49
and recognizing that
-2
is worse than
-2\phi_{\rm adv}
completes the proof.
∎

Incorporating all improvements, we have the following theorem which explicitly tracks all expressions.

Theorem 55
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{L}
be a fidelity
\phi_{\rm adv}
(as defined in Definition
47
)
(1,1)
-query sequential processes with given access to the first system of a bipartite state
\rho_{DE}
and outputting
\vec{z}_{1},\dots,\vec{z}_{L}
. Denote the event that the protocol in Figure
7
succeeds as
\Omega
. If
n\geq 50
,
n\leq 10^{6}
, then the protocol either aborts with probability at least
1-\varepsilon_{\rm accept}
, or

H_{\rm min}^{\varepsilon_{\rm smooth}}(\vec{Z}^{L}|\vec{U}^{L}E)_{\mathcal{M}_{L}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{M}_{1}(\rho)|\Omega}\geq Lh(s^{*})-\sqrt{L}c-c^{\prime},

(V.60)

where

\displaystyle h(s)=

\displaystyle n\left\{\left(t(s)-\Phi_{\rm C}\right)\cdot\left[1-\frac{(k+\ell)^{2}}{N}\right]-\phi_{\rm adv}C\right\}-2

\displaystyle t(s)=

\displaystyle\frac{s\cdot(1-\frac{1}{N^{\frac{1}{3}}})-1-dx_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min}+e^{x_{\rm min}-x_{\rm max}}}{1-e^{x_{\rm min}-x_{\rm max}}(1+x_{\rm max}-x_{\rm min})}

\displaystyle C=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left(\log_{2}n+3\right)+4\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}(n\ln 2+3.5)+1.001\times\frac{16\ln^{2}N}{N}+0.001

\displaystyle x_{\rm max}=

\displaystyle N(1-N^{-1/3})p_{\rm max}

\displaystyle x_{\rm min}=

\displaystyle N(1-N^{-1/3})p_{\rm min}

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle(1-(1+Np_{\rm min})e^{-Np_{\rm min}})-d

\displaystyle d=

\displaystyle\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C})

\displaystyle d_{\rm Q}=

\displaystyle N^{-1/3}+(k+\ell)^{2}/N+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{2^{n/2}}+\sqrt{2\left(1-\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{2k}\right)^{k}\right)}+2/\sqrt{\ell+1}+\frac{2(k+\ell)(k+\ell-1)}{N}+\frac{2k}{N(1-k/N)}

\displaystyle c=

\displaystyle V\sqrt{(2\ln 2)\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{\rm smooth}^{2}\varepsilon_{\rm accept}^{2}}}

\displaystyle c^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\frac{2\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{\rm smooth}^{2}\varepsilon_{\rm accept}^{2}}}{V^{2}\ln 2}K

\displaystyle K=

\displaystyle 4\times(w+1)^{3}\times 2^{\frac{w\sqrt{2\log_{2}\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{\rm smooth}^{2}\varepsilon_{\rm accept}^{2}}}}{V\sqrt{n\ln 2}}}

\displaystyle V=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathrm{var}+2}+\log_{2}(2\times 2^{n}+1)

\displaystyle w=

\displaystyle 2n+h(Np_{\rm max})-h(0)

\displaystyle\mathrm{var}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}}q^{\prime}(x)(h(Np_{\rm max})-h(x))^{2}dx-(h(Np_{\rm max})-h(q^{\prime}))^{2}

\displaystyle h(q^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x_{\rm max}}q^{\prime}(x)h(x)dx

\displaystyle q^{\prime}(x)=

\displaystyle\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C})\cdot\delta(x)+e^{-x}\cdot I(x)+\left[e^{-x_{\rm max}}-\max(d_{\rm Q},d_{\rm C})\right]\cdot\delta(x-x_{\rm max})

\displaystyle I(x)=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}1\penalty\ \mathrm{if}\penalty\ 0<x<x_{\rm max}\\
0\penalty\ \mathrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

any natural number
k,\ell\leq\sqrt{N}=2^{n/2}
.

We can observe the effect of the above improvements in Fig.
8
. Being able to obtain entropy at small
\gamma
means that the protocol is more robust against stronger adversaries. For example, when the adversary power is increased to 10 times Aurora, only incorporating both improvements achieves non-zero entropy.

Figure 8:
Entropy rate at different values of validation probability
\gamma
for various entropy bounds.
a
,
n=64,m=1,L=100,000,\phi=0.6,\phi_{\rm adv}=0.65,\varepsilon_{\rm accept}=d_{\rm C}=\varepsilon=10^{-4},T_{\rm QC}=50
ms, adversary’s classical simulation algorithm is 4 times as powerful as Aurora and the quantum device has
T=1
oracle access, the total validation budget is 300k Aurora node hours.
b
, same as
a
but for 15 times Aurora adversary. The unimproved version uses Theorem
48
. The reduced maximum version only improves with the single round bound from Theorem
49
. The improved variance only version only improves with the reduction in variance from Lemma
51
. The version with both improvements uses Corollary
55
. In all cases, for each
\gamma
, the hardness of the challenge circuits is set to be the total validation budget divided by
100,000\times\gamma
, the average number of validated circuits.

VI
Randomness amplification

The protocol described in Secs.
III
and
V
is for randomness
expansion
: it takes near-perfect randomness (randomness that is negligibly close to uniform in statistical distance) as input and produces more of it as output. This leads to a circularity and, in practice, we often only have
weak sources
that produce imperfect or biased randomness. Randomness
amplification
converts such imperfect or biased randomness into near-perfect randomness. While this is impossible classically without strong assumptions on the source
Santha and Vazirani 1986
, it becomes possible with quantum methods. In this section, we extend our protocol to achieve certified randomness amplification.

To perform randomness amplification rather than expansion, we must address two challenges arising from the lack of near-perfect randomness as a resource: executing the protocol itself and extracting randomness from its outputs.

•

One does not have near-perfect randomness for generating circuits to challenge the quantum computer, nor to select the samples to verify. In Sec.
VI.1
, we show that imperfect randomness suffices for challenge circuit generation. In Sec.
VI.2
, we propose methods for generating computationally indistinguishable randomness and show it is sufficient for both measurement-basis and verification-sample selection.

•

One does not have access to near-perfect randomness to perform randomness extraction. This necessitates the use of a
two-source extractor
. However, this is challenging because quantum computer responses often have very low entropy rates (for instance, in
Liu et al. 2025
the certified entropy rate is roughly
0.04
). Typical two-source extractors require
\tfrac{k_{1}}{n_{1}}+\tfrac{k_{2}}{n_{2}}>1
, where
\tfrac{k_{i}}{n_{i}}
is the min-entropy rate of the
i
th source, which would imply an unrealistically strong assumption (
k_{1}\approx n_{1}
) on the weak source if relying on prior extractor constructions. Even when the entropy rate of the quantum responses become higher (say
\frac{k_{1}}{n_{1}}>0.5
), this would still require assuming
\frac{k_{2}}{n_{2}}>1-\frac{k_{1}}{n_{1}}
on the weak source. To address this, we use the recently developed efficient implementation of Raz’s extractor
Foreman et al. 2025a
with improved parameters.

We assume access to a weak source that is a
(n,k)
block min-entropy source, producing
n
-bit blocks with min-entropy
k
, conditioned on the adversary’s side information
E
and on all previously generated blocks. Such source is sufficient to perform randomness extraction and to select the unitaries, as we describe later. For the measurement basis and for the selection of samples for verification, however, we only require pseudo randomness. The idea is that the randomness used for the measurement bases and select verification samples only needs to be unpredictable
during the protocol execution only
against the adversaries we consider. Overall, in addition to being a min-entropy source, we also assume that the weak source of randomness is computationally indistinguishable from a source that can be extracted from, a condition that only needs to hold temporarily. The protocol is schematized in Fig.
10
.

VI.1
Pseudorandom unitary with imperfectly random keys

Unfortunately, the current theory does not readily permit the use of pseudorandom unitaries generated from imperfectly random keys, as the existing security analyses assumes ideal randomness as input. For the ensemble of challenge circuits to be suitable for certified randomness,
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
argues that it is sufficient for the ensemble to be quantum statistical zero-knowledge (QSZK) indistinguishable from the Haar measure. We prove that with imperfect randomness as input, the assumption that there exists pseudorandom unitaries QSZK-indistinguishable from the Haar measure holds relative to a random oracle. Specifically, we generalize Assumption 8.8 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
by allowing the input
k
to come from some probability distribution
\mathcal{D}
of
k
that has min-entropy
O(\ell)
as opposed to the uniform distribution used in Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
.

Assumption 56

(Generalization of Assumption 8.8 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, pseudorandom unitary assumption)
.

Let
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter,
\ell,m
be polynomially bounded functions. There exists a family of keyed unitaries
\{U_{k}\in\mathbb{U}(2^{m(\kappa)}):k\in\{0,1\}^{\ell(\kappa)}\}
such that the following conditions hold:

1.

There exists a polynomial-time quantum algorithm
G
that implements
U_{k}
on input
k\in\{0,1\}^{\ell(\kappa)}
, i.e., on input
k
and quantum state
|\psi\rangle\in\mathbb{S}(2^{m(\kappa)}),G(k,|\psi\rangle)=U_{k}(\psi)
.

2.

For every pair of quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
that makes
\mathrm{poly}(\kappa)
queries and probability distribution
\mathcal{D}
of
k
that has min-entropy
O(\ell)
, it holds that

\bigg|\underset{k\sim D}{\mathbb{E}}\|\mathcal{A}^{U_{k}}-\mathcal{B}^{U_{k}}\|_{1}-\underset{U\sim\mathrm{Haar}(2^{m(\kappa)})}{\mathbb{E}}\|\mathcal{A}^{U}-\mathcal{B}^{U}\|_{1}\bigg|\leq\mathrm{negl}(\kappa).

(VI.1)

Here,
\mathbb{U}(d)
is the set of all unitaries on a Hilbert space with dimension
d
and
\mathbb{S}(d)
is the set of all statevectors with dimension
d
.

We use the same construction relative to a oracle as in
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
;
Kretschmer 2021b
. Namely,
\mathcal{O}=\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}}|k\rangle\langle k|\otimes\mathcal{O}_{k}
where
\mathcal{O}_{k}\in\mathrm{Haar}(N)
is a Haar random unitary for every
k\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
. Let
\mu=\mathrm{Haar}(N)^{2^{\ell}}
denote the measure of
\mathcal{O}
. To proceed, we modify the definition of the advantage we aim to upper bound in light of this new distribution of the keys
k
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathrm{adv}(\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{k\sim\mathcal{D},U\sim\mathrm{Haar}(N)}{\mathbb{E}}f(\mathcal{O},k,U)

\displaystyle=\underset{k\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\|\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}_{k}}-\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}_{k}}\|_{1}-\underset{U\sim\mathrm{Haar}(N)}{\mathbb{E}}\|\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O},U}-\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{O},U}\|_{1},

(VI.2)

which is also
8T
-Lipschitz due to Lemma 8.10 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
. Now we aim to upper bound this advantage by modifying subsequent lemmas. Specifically,
F
is as defined in
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
.

Lemma 57

(Generalization of Lemma 8.11 and 8.12 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be a
T
-query algorithm,
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution of
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell(\kappa)}
with min-entropy greater than
\alpha\ell
, and
F_{0}
be the zero function. Then

\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\|\mathcal{A}^{F_{x}}-\mathcal{A}^{F_{0}}\|_{1}\leq 4T2^{-\alpha\ell/2},

(VI.3)

and

\bigg|\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\|\mathcal{A}^{F_{x}}-\mathcal{B}^{F_{x}}\|_{1}-\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\|\mathcal{A}^{F_{0}}-\mathcal{B}^{F_{0}}\|_{1}\bigg|\leq 8T2^{-\alpha\ell/2}.

(VI.4)

Proof.

Everything is identical to the proof of Lemma 8.11 and 8.12 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
except Eq. 263. Instead, we have

\displaystyle\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\|\mathcal{A}^{F_{x}}-\mathcal{A}^{F_{0}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 4\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|P_{x}|\psi_{t}^{0}\rangle\|\right]=4\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{\langle\psi_{t}^{0}|P_{x}|\psi_{t}^{0}\rangle}\right]=4\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sqrt{p_{t}^{x}}\right]=4\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\sum_{x}\mathcal{D}(x)\sqrt{p_{t}^{x}},

(VI.5)

where
\mathcal{D}(x)
is the probability of obtaining
x
from distribution
\mathcal{D}
and
p_{t}^{x}\equiv\langle\psi_{t}^{0}|P_{x}|\psi_{t}^{0}\rangle
. By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\left(\sum_{x}\mathcal{D}(x)\sqrt{p_{t}^{x}}\right)^{2}\leq\left(\sum_{x}\mathcal{D}(x)^{2}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{x}p_{t}^{x}\right)=\sum_{x}\mathcal{D}(x)^{2},

(VI.6)

where we used the fact that
\sum_{x}p_{t}^{x}=\sum_{x}\langle\psi_{t}^{0}|x\rangle\langle x|\psi_{t}^{0}\rangle=1
. Since
\mathcal{D}(x)\leq 2^{-\alpha\ell}
for all
x
due to the min-entropy bound on
\mathcal{D}
,
\sum_{x}\mathcal{D}(x)^{2}
is maximized when
\mathcal{D}(x)=2^{-\alpha\ell}
for as many
x
as possible. The number of such
x
is at most
2^{\alpha\ell}
, and
\sum_{x}\mathcal{D}(x)^{2}\leq 2^{\alpha\ell}\left(2^{-\alpha\ell}\right)^{2}=2^{-\alpha\ell}
, which implies

\underset{x\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\|\mathcal{A}^{F_{x}}-\mathcal{A}^{F_{0}}\|_{1}\leq 4\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\sum_{x}\mathcal{D}(x)\sqrt{p_{t}^{x}}=4T\sqrt{\sum_{x}\mathcal{D}(x)^{2}}\leq 4T2^{-\alpha\ell/2}.

(VI.7)

This results in the first result. Following the same proof as Lemma 8.12 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
yields the second result.
∎

Lemma 58

(Generalization of Lemma 8.13 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

For every pair of
T
-query algorithms
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
,
|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{O}\sim\mu}\mathrm{adv}(\mathcal{O})|\leq 8T2^{-\alpha\ell/2}
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to Lemma 8.13 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
.
∎

Theorem 59

(Generalization of Theorem 8.15 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

For
\alpha\ell=\lceil n-\log_{2}n\rceil
and
L=2^{\ell}
,
\Pr_{\mathcal{O}\sim\mu}[|\mathrm{adv}(\mathcal{O})|\geq 16TL^{-\alpha}]\leq 2^{-\Omega(n)}
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to Theorem 8.15 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
. Note that there is a typo in Eq. 271 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
when applying the constants.
∎

One undesirable feature of the above theorem is that it requires the key length to be precisely
\lceil n-\log_{2}n\rceil
. Intuitively, increasing the key length should only improve the indistinguishability. We choose a slightly different parameterization for a more desirable result.

Theorem 60
.

For
\alpha\ell\geq n
,
\Pr_{\mathcal{O}\sim\mu}[|\mathrm{adv}(\mathcal{O})|\geq 2^{-\Omega(n)}]\leq 2^{-\Omega(n)}
.

Proof.

By the same argument as Theorem 8.15 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
, Theorem 8.14 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
implies

\Pr_{\mathcal{O}\sim\mu}\left[\mathrm{adv}(\mathcal{O})\geq\underset{\mathcal{P}\sim\mu}{\mathbb{E}}[\mathrm{adv}(\mathcal{P})]+t\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{(N-2)t^{2}}{24K^{2}}\right).

(VI.8)

Choosing
t=n^{1/2}2^{-n/2}
, by Lemma
58
and the aforementioned fact that
\mathrm{adv}(\mathcal{O})
is
8T
-Lipschitz,

\Pr_{\mathcal{O}\sim\mu}\left[\mathrm{adv}(\mathcal{O})\geq 8T2^{-\alpha\ell/2}+n^{1/2}2^{-n/2}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{(N-2)n2^{-n}}{1536T^{2}}\right).

(VI.9)

∎

The following corollary similarly holds.

Corollary 61

(Generalization of Corollary 8.16 of Ref.
Aaronson and Hung 2023b
)
.

There exists an oracle relative to which Assumption
56
holds with probability
1
.

VI.2
Uplifting imperfect randomness to computationally indistinguishable from uniform randomness

We now describe how we obtain computational randomness suitable for generating random measurement bases in our protocol, as well as for selecting the samples to be verified. At a high level, this involves deriving from the weak source a seed that is computationally indistinguishable from uniform, and then optionally expanding it using a cryptographically secure pseudo-random number generator (CS-PRNG). We note that the PRNG is not strictly needed, as we can use a sufficiently long seed directly (which is what we do in the experiment). To formalize this, we introduce the notion of
pseudosources
, inspired by the pseudoentropy framework of Ref.
Håstad et al. 1999
, which imposes requirements only against computationally bounded distinguishers. We begin by defining computational indistinguishability, pseudosources, and CS-PRNGs.

Definition 62

(Computational distinguishability)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of (possibly randomized) functions representing the admissible distinguishers. The computational distinguishability between two random variables
X\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
Y\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, with respect to
\mathcal{C}
, is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{comp\mathchar 45\relax dist}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y):=

\displaystyle\sup_{D\in\mathcal{C}}\left|\Pr[D(X)=1]-\Pr[D(Y)=1]\right|

(VI.10)

where
\Pr[D(\cdot)=1]
denotes the probability that the function
D
outputs
1
on input drawn from
\cdot
.

In our case, we assume access to additional (classical) side information, the random variable
E
, to help with this distinguishing.

Definition 63

(Conditional computational distinguishability)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of (possibly randomized) functions available to an adversary, and let
E
be a random variable representing the adversary’s side information. Let
X,Y\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
be random variables that may be correlated with
E
. The computational distinguishability of
X
and
Y
conditioned on
E
with respect to
\mathcal{C}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{comp\mathchar 45\relax dist}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y|E):=\sup_{D\in\mathcal{C}}\left|\Pr[D(X,E)=1]-\Pr[D(Y,E)=1]\right|,

(VI.11)

where the probabilities are taken over the joint distributions of
(X,E)
and
(Y,E)
respectively, as well as the internal randomness of
D
(if randomized).

Using this, we can define a pseudosource.

Definition 64

(Pseudosource)
.

A random variable
X
is a
(\mathcal{C},\varepsilon_{\rm source})
-pseudosource for a random variable
Y
if, for all distinguishers
D\in\mathcal{C}
and side information
E
, the advantage of
D
to distinguishing between
X
and
Y
is at most
\varepsilon_{\rm source}
, i.e.
\mathrm{comp\mathchar 45\relax dist}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y|E)\leq\varepsilon_{\rm source}
.

While CS-PRNGs are typically defined without side information, their security naturally extends to the case with side information independent the seed, which we adopt below.

Definition 65

(CS-PRNG with side information)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the class of functions computable in polynomial time. A deterministic function
\mathrm{PRF}:\{0,1\}^{\ell}\to\{0,1\}^{n_{\rm PRF}}
is a CS-PRNG if
n_{\rm PRF}>\ell
and for any random variable
E
independent of
U_{s}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{comp\mathchar 45\relax dist}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{PRF}(U_{\ell}),U_{n_{\rm PRF}}|E)\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PRF}},

(VI.12)

where
U_{\ell}
and
U_{n_{\rm PRF}}
are uniform distributions over
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
\{0,1\}^{n_{\rm PRF}}
, respectively, and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PRF}}
is negligible in
n_{\rm PRF}
.

To use a CS-PRNG, we require a seed consisting of uniform and independent bits. However, in randomness amplification, we only have access to a weak source. To obtain randomness that is computationally indistinguishable from uniform, we must first perform randomness extraction. Although there are several choices, we make one of the weakest possible assumptions, namely that our
(n,k)
block min-entropy source is a
[(n_{1},k_{1}){,}(n_{2},k_{2})]
Markov pseudosource. In the main text we referred to the weak source being computationally indistinguishable from two independent sources, we now relax this condition to the weaker Markov independence. In words, Markov independence allows the two sources to be correlated through a common cause (the adversary’s side-information in our case) and the independence condition now only needs to hold when conditioned on this common cause.

Formally, Markov pseudosource are defined as follows:

Definition 66

(Markov source)
.

The random variables
(Y_{1},Y_{2})
is a
[(n_{1},k_{1}){,}(n_{2},k_{2})]
Markov source, conditioned on side information
E
, if
Y_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{n_{1}}
,
Y_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n_{2}}
, and

\displaystyle H_{\rm min}(Y_{1}|E)

\displaystyle=k_{1}

\displaystyle H_{\rm min}(Y_{2}|E)

\displaystyle=k_{2}

\displaystyle I(Y_{1}{:}Y_{2}|E)

\displaystyle=0,

where
H_{\min}(Y_{1}|E)
denotes the min-entropy of
Y_{1}
given
E
, and
I(Y_{1}{:}Y_{2}|E)
the mutual information between
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
conditioned on
E
.

Definition 67

(Markov pseudosource)
.

The random variables
(X_{1},X_{2})
are a
[(n_{1},k_{1}){,}(n_{2},k_{2})]
Markov
(\mathcal{C},\varepsilon_{\rm source})
-pseudosource if there exists an
[(n_{1},k_{1}){,}(n_{2},k_{2})]
Markov source
(Y_{1},Y_{2})
conditioned on
E
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{comp\mathchar 45\relax dist}_{\mathcal{C}}((X_{1},X_{2}),(Y_{1},Y_{2})|E)\leq\varepsilon_{\rm source}.

(VI.13)

Markov sources can be extracted by two-source extractors secure in the Markov model;

Definition 68

(Definition 5 of Ref.
Arnon-Friedman et al. 2016
)
.

A function
\mathrm{Ext}:\{0,1\}^{n_{1}}\times\{0,1\}^{n_{2}}\to\{0,1\}^{m}
is a classical-proof
(n_{1},k_{1},n_{2},k_{2},m,\varepsilon)
two-source extractor secure in the Markov model if the following holds: For any
[(n_{1},k_{1}){,}(n_{2},k_{2})]
Markov source
(Y_{1},Y_{2})
conditioned on classical side information
E
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta((\mathrm{Ext}(Y_{1},Y_{2}),E),(U_{m},E))\leq\varepsilon,

(VI.14)

where
U_{m}
denotes the uniform distribution over
\{0,1\}^{m}
and
\Delta(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the statistical distance. We say that
\mathrm{Ext}
is strong in the
i
th input (for
i\in\{1,2\}
) if

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathrm{Ext}(Y_{1},Y_{2}),Y_{i},E),(U_{m},Y_{i},E))\leq\varepsilon.

(VI.15)

As one might expect, any two-source extractor remains secure against computationally bounded distinguishers, as shown in the following lemma.

Lemma 69

(Two-source extractors are secure against computational distinguishers)
.

Let
\mathrm{Ext}:\{0,1\}^{n_{1}}\times\{0,1\}^{n_{2}}\to\{0,1\}^{m}
be a classical-proof
(n_{1},k_{1},n_{2},k_{2},m,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ext}})
two-source extractor secure in the Markov model. Then for every
[(n_{1},k_{1}){,}(n_{2},k_{2})]
Markov source, conditioned on
E
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{comp\mathchar 45\relax dist}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{Ext}(Y_{1},Y_{2}),U_{m}|E)\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ext}}.

(VI.16)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{comp\mathchar 45\relax dist}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{Ext}(Y_{1},Y_{2}),U_{m}|E)

(VI.17)

\displaystyle=\sup_{D\in\mathcal{C}}\left|\Pr[D(\mathrm{Ext}(Y_{1},Y_{2}),E)=1]-\Pr[D(U_{m},E)=1]\right|

(VI.18)

\displaystyle=\sup_{D\in\mathcal{C}}\left|\sum_{z,e}\delta_{D(z,e)}^{1}\Pr[\mathrm{Ext}(Y_{1},Y_{2})=z,E=e]-\sum_{z,e}\delta_{D(z,e)}^{1}\Pr[U_{m}=z,E=e]\right|

(VI.19)

\displaystyle=\sup_{D\in\mathcal{C}}\left|\sum_{z,e}\delta_{D(z,e)}^{1}\left(\Pr[\mathrm{Ext}(Y_{1},Y_{2})=z,E=e]-\Pr[U_{m}=z,E=e]\right)\right|

(VI.20)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{z,e}\max(\Pr[\mathrm{Ext}(Y_{1},Y_{2})=z,E=e]-\Pr[U_{m}=z,E=e],0)\right|

(VI.21)

\displaystyle=\Delta((\mathrm{Ext}(Y_{1},Y_{2}),E),(U_{m},E))\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ext}}

(VI.22)

where
\delta_{x}^{y}
denotes the Kronecker delta which is
1
if
x=y
and
0
otherwise,
\Delta
denotes the statistical distance, and the final inequality follows from the security guarantee of two-source extractors in the Markov model.
∎

We will also use the fact that applying any function contained in
\mathcal{C}
does not increase the computational distinguishability. This is shown in the following lemma.

Lemma 70
.

For any function
\mathrm{f}
computable in
\mathcal{C}
, if

\displaystyle\mathrm{comp\mathchar 45\relax dist}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y|E)\leq\varepsilon,

(VI.23)

then

\displaystyle\mathrm{comp\mathchar 45\relax dist}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{f}(X),\mathrm{f}(Y)|E)\leq\varepsilon.

(VI.24)

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that there exists a distinguishing algorithm
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
such that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[D^{\prime}(\mathrm{f}(X),E)=1]-\Pr[D^{\prime}(\mathrm{f}(Y),E)=1]\right|>\varepsilon.

(VI.25)

Define a distinguishing algorithm
D\in\mathcal{C}
by
D(X,E):=D^{\prime}(\mathrm{f}(X),E)
.
Then

\displaystyle\left|\Pr[D(X,E)=1]-\Pr[D(Y,E)=1]\right|=\left|\Pr[D^{\prime}(\mathrm{f}(X),E)=1]-\Pr[D^{\prime}(\mathrm{f}(Y),E)=1]\right|>\varepsilon,

(VI.26)

contradicting
\mathrm{comp\mathchar 45\relax dist}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y|E)\leq\varepsilon
.
∎

Together, these results can be used to obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 71
.

Let
\mathrm{Ext}:\{0,1\}^{n_{1}}\times\{0,1\}^{n_{2}}\to\{0,1\}^{m}
be any classical-proof two-source
(n_{1},k_{1},n_{2},k_{2},m,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ext}})
-extractor secure in the Markov model and computable in
\mathcal{C}
,

\mathrm{PRF}:\{0,1\}^{m}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
a CS-PRNG secure against
\mathcal{C}
, computable in
\mathcal{C}
, with distinguishing advantage
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PRF}}
, and
(X_{1},X_{2})
any
[(n_{1},k_{1}){,}(n_{2},k_{2})]
Markov
(\mathcal{C},\varepsilon_{\rm source})
-pseudosource. Then,

\displaystyle\text{comp-dist}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{PRF}(\mathrm{Ext}(X_{1},X_{2})),U_{n}|E)\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{PRF}}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ext}}+\varepsilon_{\rm source}.

(VI.27)

Proof.

Using the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\text{comp-dist}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{PRF}(\mathrm{Ext}(X_{1},X_{2})),U_{n}|E)

(VI.28)

\displaystyle=\sup_{D\in\mathcal{C}}\left|\Pr[D(\mathrm{PRF}(\mathrm{Ext}(X_{1},X_{2})),E)=1]-\Pr[D(U_{n},E)=1]\right|

(VI.29)
